&=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(ij)}\right]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}^{(i)}\right]}{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}\\
&=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{p_{j}}{1-p_{i}}{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}\end{split}

It then follows that :

\displaystyle{\rm AUC}^{\rm{ovo}}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{N_{C}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}{\rm{AUC}}_{i|\neg i}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{1}{N_{C}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{p_{j}}{1-p_{i}}{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}.

Proof of (b)
.
When
p_{i}=\frac{1}{N_{C}},~\forall i=1,2,\cdots,N_{C}
, we have
\frac{p_{j}}{1-p_{i}}=\frac{p_{i}}{1-p_{j}}=\frac{1}{N_{C}-1}
, which completes the proof with the conclusion of (a).

Proof of (c)
.
Since
{\rm AUC}^{\rm{ovo}}
and
{{\rm AUC}^{\rm{ova}}}
are convex combinations of
{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}
, and that
{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}\in[0,1]
, we have

{\rm AUC}^{\rm{ovo}}=1\leftrightarrow{{\rm AUC}_{i|j}}=1,~\forall i\neq j\leftrightarrow{{\rm AUC}^{\rm{ova}}}=1.

∎

Appendix B
Consistency Analysis

In this section, we will provide all the proofs for Consistency Analysis.

B-A

The Bayes Optimal Scoring Function

First, we show the derivation of the Bayes optimal scoring function set under the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
criterion.

Restate of Theorem
2
.

Given
\eta_{i}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}[y=i|x]
,
p_{i}=\mathbb{P}[y=i]
, we have the following consequences:

(a)

f=\{f^{(i)}\}_{i=1,2,\cdots,N_{C}}\in\mathcal{F}^{N_{C}}_{\sigma}
is a Bayes optimal scoring function under the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
criterion, if:

\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\cdot\left(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\right)>0,~\forall\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\neq\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}),

where:

\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{2})}{2p_{i}p_{j}},~~\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{1})\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})}{2p_{i}p_{j}}.\\

(b)

Define
\sigma(\cdot)
as the sigmoid function,
s_{i}(\bm{x})=\eta_{i}(\bm{x})/p_{i}
and
s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x})=\sum_{j\neq i}s_{j}(\bm{x})
, then a Bayesian scoring function could be given by:

f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x})=\begin{cases}\sigma\left(\dfrac{s_{i}(\bm{x})}{s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x})}\right),&0\leq\eta_{i}(\bm{x})<1\\
1,&\eta_{i}(\bm{x})=1.\end{cases}

(10)

Proof
.

dasdsa

Proof of
(a)

First let us summarize the notations we will use in the forthcoming derivation in Tab.
V
.

TABLE V
:
Notations and descriptions.

Notation

Description

\mathcal{Y}_{ij}

is the event
[y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j]

\mathcal{E}_{i,j}

is the event
\mathcal{Y}_{ij}~\text{or}~\mathcal{Y}_{ji}

y^{(i)}_{1}

=1
if
y_{1}=i
, otherwise
y^{(i)}_{1}=0

y^{(i)}_{2}

=1
if
y_{2}=i
, otherwise
y^{(i)}_{2}=0

\bm{x}_{1,2}

refers to the couple
(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})

y_{1,2}

refers to the couple
(y_{1},y_{2})

\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})

is event that
(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2}))\cdot(y^{(i)}_{1}-y^{(i)}_{2})<0

\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((1,0),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)

is event that
(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2}))\cdot(1-0)<0

\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((0,1),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)

is event that
(f^{(i)}\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2}))\cdot(0-1)<0

\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})

is the event that
f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})=f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})

\eta_{i}(\bm{x})

\mathbb{P}(y=i|\bm{x})

\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})

\sum_{j\neq i}(\frac{\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{2})}{2p_{i}p_{j}})

\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})

\sum_{j\neq i}(\frac{\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{1})\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})}{2p_{i}p_{j}})

First of all, we prove that the condition holds for:

f\in\arginf_{f\in\mathcal{M}}R(f)

where

\mathcal{M}=\{f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(N_{C})}),f^{(i)}~\text{is a measurable function}.\}

First of all, we can remove the dependence on the conditioning of
\mathcal{E}_{i,j}
. We have

\begin{split}N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\bigg(\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})|\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})|\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]\bigg)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\right]}+\frac{1}{2}\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]}{\mathbb{P}\left[\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\right]}\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]}{{2p_{i}p_{j}}}\end{split}

(11)

Now we only need to expand the joint possibility by expectation, which leads to:

\begin{split}\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\big]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}&=\dfrac{\expe_{\bm{x}_{1,2},y_{1,2}}\big[\bm{I}[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})]\cdot\bm{I}[\mathcal{E}_{i,j}]\big]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}\\
&=\dfrac{\expe_{\bm{x}_{1,2}}\bigg[\expe_{y_{1,2}|\bm{x}_{1,2}}\big[\bm{I}[\mathcal{G}_{i}(y_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})]\cdot\bm{I}[\mathcal{E}_{i,j}]\big]\bigg]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}\\
&=\dfrac{\expe_{\bm{x}_{1,2}}\bigg[\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((1,0),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)]+\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((0,1),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)]\bigg]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}.\end{split}

(12)

Similarly, we have:

\dfrac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2}),\mathcal{E}_{i,j})\big]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}=\dfrac{\expe_{\bm{x}_{1,2}}\bigg[\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})\big]+\eta_{j}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})\big]\bigg]}{2p_{i}p_{j}}

(13)

Above all, by combining Eq.(
11
)-Eq.(
13
), we reach that :

\begin{split}N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}=&\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\expe_{x_{1,2}}\bigg[\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((1,0),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)\big]+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}))\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((0,1),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)\big]\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\big)\cdot I\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})\big]\bigg].\end{split}

(14)

Fix a sub-scoring function
f^{(i)}
and
\bm{x}_{1,2}
, we only need to minimize the following quantity
L^{(i)}
to reach the Bayes optimal solution:

L^{(i)}=\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((1,0),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)\big]+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}))\cdot\bm{I}\big[\mathcal{G}_{i}\left((0,1),\bm{x}_{1,2}\right)\big]+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\big)\cdot I\big[\mathcal{G}_{0,i}(\bm{x}_{1,2})\big]

(15)

Obviously
,

L^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})=\begin{cases}\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}),&f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})>0,\\
\\
\dfrac{\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})}{2},&f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})=0,\\
\\
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}),&f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})<0.\end{cases}

(16)

To obtain the Bayes optimal scoring function, we must minimize
L(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
for any
\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
.

When
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})=\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
,
L(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
stays as a constant, which is irrelevant to the choice of
f^{(i)}
.

When
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})<\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
, we must have
f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})<0
to minimize
L(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
.

When
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
, we must have
f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})>0
to minimize
L(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
.

Above all, by choosing
f^{(i)}
such that
\forall\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X},\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\neq\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})

\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\cdot\left(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\right)>0,

one can reach the minimum of
R^{(i)}(f^{(i)})
as
\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}{\min\{\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}),\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\}}.

This ends the proof of the condition for:

f\in\arginf_{f\in\mathcal{M}}R(f)

To extend the conclusion to:

f\in\arginf_{f\in\mathcal{F}^{N_{C}}_{\sigma}}R(f)

One only need to notice that if
f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})>0
, then for any strictly increasing measurable function
\varphi
, we have:

\varphi(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1}))-\varphi(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2}))>0
. Obvisously, a similar result holds for
f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})<0
. This implies if
f\in\mathcal{M}
is Bayes optimal, then

\varphi\circ f=(\varphi\circ f^{(1)},\cdots,\varphi\circ f^{(N_{C})})

is also Bayes optimal with
\varphi\circ f\in\mathcal{F}^{N_{C}}_{\sigma}
. The proof is then completed.

Proof of
(b)

Fixing class
i
, if
\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{k})\neq 1,~k=1,2
, then by dividing
s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}_{2})
in Eq.(
2
) in Thm.
2
, we find that
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x})
could be chosen as
\sigma(s_{i}(\bm{x})/s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}))
.

Otherwise, if
\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})=1,\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})<1
(note that
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\neq\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
) then
s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}_{1})=0
. To reach
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})>f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})
, we can set
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})=1
,
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})=\sigma\left(s_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})/s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)
.

In the last case, we have
\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})<1,\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{2})=1
, we can reach that an optimal solution could be
f(\bm{x}_{1})=\sigma\left(s_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})/s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}_{1})\right),f(\bm{x}_{2})=1
.

Since
\bm{x}_{1}
,
\bm{x}_{2}
is arbitrarily chosen, a Bayesian scoring function could be then set as:
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x})=\sigma(s_{i}(\bm{x})/s_{\backslash i}(\bm{x}))
(
<1
) if
\eta_{i}(\bm{x})<1
;
f^{\star(i)}(\bm{x})=1
otherwise.
∎

B-B

Consistency Analysis of Surrogate Losses

Based on Thm.
2
, we provide the following result for some popular surrogate losses.

Restate of Theorem
3
.

The surrogate loss
\ell
is consistent with
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
if it is differentiable, convex, nonincreasing within
[-1,1]
and
\ell^{\prime}(0)<0
.

Proof
.

First, recall that we have the following definition for the surrogate risk:

\begin{split}&R_{\ell}(f)=\sum_{i}\frac{R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\\
&R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})=\sum_{j\neq i}\expe_{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2}}\left[{\ell(\Delta(y^{(i)}_{1,2})\Delta{f}^{(i)})|\mathcal{E}^{(ij)}}\right],\end{split}

where
R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})
is the corresponding risk for
f^{(i)}
.

Denote:

f^{\star}=\arginf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R_{\ell}(f)

with
f^{\star}=(f^{\star(1)},f^{\star(2)},\cdots,f^{\star(N_{C})})
.

Moreover, it is obvious that:

\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R_{\ell}(f)=\frac{1}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)}\cdot\sum_{i}\inf_{f^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)}).

(17)

In this sense, each binary scoring function
f^{\star(i)}
could be solved independently for different subproblems,
i.e.

f^{\star(i)}=\arginf_{f^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})

Similar to the derivation in Thm.
2
, we could rewrite
R^{(i)}_{\ell}
as:

R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\int_{\mathcal{X}}\left[\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\cdot\ell(f^{(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{(i)}\left(x^{\prime}\right))+\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})\cdot\ell(f^{(i)}\left(\bm{x}^{\prime}\right)-f^{(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right))\right]d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}^{\prime})

where

\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})=\frac{\eta_{i}(\bm{x})}{p_{i}},~\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\eta_{j}(\bm{x})}{p_{j}}.

Denote
\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}
as the set of
f^{(i)}
, where
f
is a Bayes optimal socring functions,
i.e.

\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}=\{f^{(i)}:\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\cdot(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}))>0,~\forall(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})~s.t.~\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\neq\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\}

Now proof the theorem with the following steps.

Claim 1
.

f^{\star(i)}\in\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}

We prove the claim by contradiction. To do this, we assume that
claim does not hold in the sense that
\exists\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}
s.t.
f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\leq f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)
but
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
.

Case 1:
\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})>0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})>0

Define an intermediate function
\delta_{h}(\gamma)=R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{\star(i)}+\gamma h)
, we must have
\delta^{\prime}_{h}(0)=0
,
\forall h
.

Given any pair of instance
(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
, with
\bm{x}_{1}\neq\bm{x}_{2}
, and for all
\ell
satisfying the sufficient condition, we prove that
f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)>f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)
must imply that
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
by contradiction.

Picking

h_{1}(x)=\frac{\bm{I}\left[x=x_{1}\right]}{\eta_{-}(\bm{x})},

using
\delta^{\prime}_{h_{1}}(0)=0
, we have:

\int_{X\backslash\bm{x}_{1}}\frac{\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{1})}{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})}\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)-\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})=0

(18)

Picking

h_{2}(x)=\frac{\bm{I}\left[x=x_{2}\right]}{\eta_{-}(\bm{x})},

using
\delta^{\prime}_{h_{2}}(0)=0
, we have:

\int_{X\backslash\bm{x}_{2}}\frac{\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{2})}{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})}\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)-\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})=0

(19)

Taking (
18
) - (
19
), we have:

\begin{split}&\underbrace{\int_{X\backslash\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\}}\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})\cdot\left(\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)-\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{(a)}}\\
&+\underbrace{\int_{X\backslash\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\}}\frac{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})}{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})}\left(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{(b)}}\\
&+\underbrace{(\frac{\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{1})}{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})}+\frac{\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{2})}{\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})})\cdot\left(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)\right)}_{\bm{(c)}}\\
&=0\end{split}

(20)

We now construct a contradiction with the equation above.

First notice that

\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)\leq\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\right)

since by assumption
\ell
is nonconvex,
i.e.
,
\ell^{\prime}
is nondecreasing. This implies
\bm{(a)}\leq 0
.

With a similar spirit, we have:

\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)\leq\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)\leq 0.

Together with
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
, we have
\bm{(b)}\leq 0
.

Now, we prove that
\bm{(c)}<0
:

Case a
: If
f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)=f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)
, we have :

\bm{(c)}=(\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})-\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1}))\cdot\ell^{\prime}(0)<0

since
\ell(0)<0
.

Case b
If
f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)<f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)
, we have:

\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)\leq\ell^{\prime}(0)<0,

\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\right)\leq 0,

and
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
, we have:
\bm{(c)}<0

Putting all togther, we have that
\bm{(a)+(b)+(c)}<0
, which contradicts with eq.(
21
). Since the choice of
(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})
is arbitrary, this shows that

f^{\star(i)}\in\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma},~\text{if}~\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})>0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})>0

Case 2:
\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})=0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})>0

Since :

\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}\left[\bm{x}_{1},y=i\right]=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{1})=\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{1})

We have :

\sum_{i}\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})=1.

This further implies that
\eta_{i}(\bm{x}_{1})
reaches its maximum 1, which means
\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{1})>\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{2})
.

Similar to the proof of Case 1, we can set
h_{1}(\bm{x})=\bm{I}\left[\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{1}\right],~h_{2}(\bm{x})=\bm{I}\left[\bm{x}=\bm{x}_{2}\right]
and obtain the following equation.

\begin{split}&\underbrace{\int_{X\backslash\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\}}\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x})\cdot\left(\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)\right)-\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{(a)}}\\
&+\underbrace{\int_{X\backslash\{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}\}}\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x})\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}\right)\right)d\mathbb{P}(\bm{x})}_{\bm{(b)}}\\
&+\underbrace{(\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{1})+\mathbb{P}(\bm{x}_{2}))\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{+}(\bm{x}_{1})\cdot\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})\cdot\ell^{\prime}\left(f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{1}\right)-f^{\star(i)}\left(\bm{x}_{2}\right)\right)}_{\bm{(c)}}\\
&=0\end{split}

(21)

Similarly, one can show that
\bm{(a)+(b)+(c)}<0
, which contradicts with the optimal condition.

f^{\star(i)}\in\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma},~\text{if}~\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})=0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})>0

Case 3:
\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})>0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})=0
.
In this sense, we have
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})<\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
, which contradicts with the assumption that
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
.
Thus this is impossible to observe.

Case 4:
\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{1})=0,\eta^{(i)}_{-}(\bm{x}_{2})=0
.
We have
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})=\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
, which contradicts with the assumption that
\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})>\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})
.
Thus this is impossible to observe.

Then the proof of Claim
1
is completed.

Claim 2
.

\inf_{f^{(i)}\notin\mathsf{Bayes}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})>\inf_{f^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})

(22)

Claim
2
follows that
\arginf_{f^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})=\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}.

Claim 3
.

For any sequence
\{f_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}
, such that
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
,

R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f_{t})\rightarrow R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{\star(i)})~\text{implies}~R^{(i)}(f_{t})\rightarrow\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}(f)

Since
\arginf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}(f)=\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}
, we only need to prove that
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}f_{t}\in\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}
. Define
\delta^{(i)}
as:

\delta^{(i)}=\inf_{f^{(i)}\notin\mathsf{Bayes}^{(i)}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})-\inf_{f^{(i)}\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)})>0

Suppose that
\lim f_{t}\notin\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}
, then for large enough
T
, we have:

R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f_{T})-R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{\star(i)})>\delta^{(i)},

which contradicts with the fact that
R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f_{t})\rightarrow R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{\star{(i)}})
. This shows that Eq.(
22
) holds.

Since the argument above is based on an arbitrarily chosen class
i
, Eq.(
22
) holds for all i.

Claim 4
.

Given a sequence
\{f\}_{t}
with
f_{t}=(f^{(1)}_{t},\cdots,f^{(N_{C})}_{t})
, we have:

R_{\ell}(f_{t})\rightarrow R_{\ell}(f)~\text{implies}~R(f_{t})\rightarrow\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\sigma}}R(f)

This directly follows that:

R(f_{t})=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}R^{(i)}(f^{(i)}_{t})}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)},~R_{\ell}(f_{t})=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}R^{(i)}_{\ell}(f^{(i)}_{t})}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)}.

and Claim
4
.

The proof is then completed according to Def.
1
. ∎

Appendix C
Unbiased Estimation of
R_{\ell}

In this section, we provide a derivation of an unbiased estimation of
R_{\ell}
.

Restate of Proposition
1
.

\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f)
as:

\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})).

\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f)
is an unbiased estimation of
{R}_{\ell}(f)
, in the sense that:

{R}_{\ell}(f)=\expe\limits_{\mathcal{S}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f))
, where
\mathcal{N}_{i}
denotes the set of all samples in
\mathcal{S}
with
y=i
.

Proof
.

\expe_{\mathcal{S}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f))=\expe_{{\bm{Y}}}\left[\expe_{\bm{X}|{\bm{Y}}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f))\right]

Moreover, we have

\begin{split}&\expe_{\bm{X}|{\bm{Y}}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f))\\
&~~~=\frac{1}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}\left[\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})|y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j\right]\\
&~~~~=\frac{1}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}\left[\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})|y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j\right].\end{split}

Since
\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}\left[\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})|y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j\right]
does not depend on the distribution of
\bm{Y}
, we have:

\expe_{{\bm{Y}}}\left[\expe_{\bm{X}|{\bm{Y}}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f))\right]=\expe_{\bm{X}|{\bm{Y}}}(\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f)).

Now it only remains to prove that

\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}\left[\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)|y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j\right]=\expe_{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2}}\left[\ell\left(\Delta(y^{(i)})\Delta(f^{(i)})\right)|\mathcal{E}^{(ij)}\right].

To see this, we have:

\begin{split}&\expe_{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2}}\left[\ell\left(\Delta(y^{(i)})\Delta(f^{(i)})\right)|\mathcal{E}^{(ij)}\right]\\
\\
&~~{=}\expe_{\bm{z}_{1},\bm{z}_{2}}{\left[\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)+\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2},\bm{x}_{1})\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})\right)\right]}\\
\\
&~~=2\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}{\left[\pi^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2})\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\right]}\\
\\
&~~{=}\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}{\left[\frac{\eta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})\eta^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{2})}{p_{i}p_{j}}\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)\right]}\\
\\
&~~{=}\expe_{\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}}\left[\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right)|y_{1}=i,y_{2}=j\right].\end{split}

The proof is thus completed.
∎

Appendix D
Preliminary for Generalization Analyses

D-A

Concentration Inequalities

In this subsection, we provide a brief introduction to the concentration bounds that are employed throughout the next two sections.

D-A
1
Bounded Difference Property

Definition 5

(Bounded Difference Property)
.

Given independent random variables
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
, with
X_{i}\in\mathbb{X}
, A function
f(X_{1},X_{2},\cdots,X_{n})
is said to has the bounded difference property if there exist non-negative constants
c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{n}
, such that:

\sup_{x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n},x^{\prime}_{i}}\left|f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})-f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},x^{\prime}_{i},\cdots,x_{n})\right|\leq c_{i},~\forall 1\leq i\leq n.

(23)

For all functions satisfying the Bounded Difference Property, we have the following Bounded Difference Inequality over the generated momentum functions.

Lemma 1

(Bounded Difference Inequality)
.

[
7
,
Prop.6.1, Thm.6.2]

Assume that
Z=f(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})
, with
X_{i}
s being independent, satisfies the bounded difference property with constants
c_{1},c_{2},\cdots,c_{n}
. Denote

v=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}^{2}

(24)

then we have:

\log\expe\left[\exp\left(\lambda(Z-\expe[Z])\right)\right]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}v^{2}}{2},

(25)

holds for all
\lambda>0
.

D-A
2
Maximal Inequality

Lemma 2

(Maximal Inequality)
.

[
7
,
Sec.2.5]
Let
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{n}
be real-valued random variables where a
v>0
exists, such that for every
i=1,2,\cdots,n
, we have
\log\big(\expe\big[\exp\left(\lambda Z_{i}\right)\big]\big)\leq\frac{\lambda v^{2}}{2}
, then we have:

\expe\left[\max_{i=1,2,\cdots,n}Z_{i}\right]\leq\sqrt{2v\log n}.

D-A
3
Mcdiarmid Inequality

First let us review the fundamental concentration inequality that is adopted in our proof.

Lemma 3

(Mcdiarmid inequality)
.

[
52
]

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{m}
be independent random variables all taking values in the set
\mathcal{X}
. Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function of

X_{1},\cdots,X_{m}
that satisfies:

\sup_{\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}}|f(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i},\cdots,x_{m})-f(x_{1},\cdots,x^{\prime}_{i}\cdots,x_{,})|\leq c_{i},

with
\bm{x}\neq\bm{x}^{\prime}
.
Then for all
\epsilon>0
,

\mathbb{P}[\mathbb{E}(f)-f\geq\epsilon]\leq\exp\left(\dfrac{-2\epsilon^{2}}{\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}^{2}}\right).

D-B

Properties of Rademacher Averages

The following lemmas are instrumental for our derivation of
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity upper bounds in Lem.
10
and Lem.
11
.

First the Khinchin-Kahane inequality bridges the
\ell_{p}
norm average to the
\ell_{q}
norm average, given that
1<p<q<\infty
.

Lemma 4

(Khinchin-Kahane inequality)
.

[
39
]

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{n}
be a family of i.i.d. Rademacher variables. Then for any
1<p<q<\infty
, we have:

\bigg(\expe_{\sigma}\left[\big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f_{i}\big|^{q}\right]\bigg)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{q}}}\leq\left(\frac{q-1}{p-1}\right)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\cdot\bigg(\expe_{\sigma}\left[\big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f_{i}\big|^{p}\right]\bigg)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}.

Dealing with the Rademacher complexities for vector-valued function, we need the vector version of the well-known Talagrand contraction lemma, see
[
51
,
14
]
for a proof.

Lemma 5

(vector version Talagrand contraction lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be any set,
n\in\mathcal{N}
,
(\bm{x}_{1};\cdots;\bm{x}_{N})\in X_{n}
, let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of k-component-functions
f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(k)}):X\rightarrow\ell_{2}^{k}
and let
h:\ell_{2}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
have Lipschitz norm
\phi
. Then,

\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}h(f(\bm{x}_{i}))\right]\leq\sqrt{2}\phi\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sigma_{i,k}(f^{(k)}(\bm{x}_{i}))\right],

where
\sigma_{1}\cdots,\sigma_{N}
, and
\sigma_{1,1},\cdots,\sigma_{1,K},\cdots,\sigma_{N,K}
are two sequences of independent Rademacher random variables.

The following Lemma is a simple extension of Lem.1 in
[
27
]
to double indexed Rademacher random sequence, which is the key to the derivation of Lem.
11
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
s
be a 1-Lipschitz, positive-homogeneous activation function which is applied element-wise. Then for any class of vector-valued functions
\mathcal{F}
, and any convex and monotonically increasing function
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, we have:

\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F},||W||_{F}\leq R_{W}}g\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\sigma_{i,c}\cdot s(\bm{W}f(\bm{x}_{i}))\right\|\right)\right]\leq 2\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}g\left(R_{W}\cdot\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\sigma_{i,c}\cdot f(\bm{x}_{i})\right\|\right)\right]

where
\{\sigma_{i,c}\}
is a sequence of double indexed Rademacher random variables.

D-C

Lipschitz Properties of Softmax

In this section, we provide an
\ell_{2}
Lipschitz Constant for softmax component function.

Lemma 7

(Lipschitz Constant for softmax components)
.

Given
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, the function
\mathsf{soft}_{i},~i=1,2,\cdots,K
is a mapping
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{X}\rightarrow[0,1]
, defined as:

\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})=\dfrac{\exp(x_{i})}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp(x_{j})},

then
\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\cdot)
is
\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}
-Lipschitz continuous with respect to vector
\ell_{2}
norm.

Proof
.

We only need to prove that

\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\big\|\nabla_{\bm{x}}\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\big\|_{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}.

For any
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have:

\frac{\partial\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})}{\partial x_{j}}=\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\cdot\left(I[i=j]-\mathsf{soft}_{j}(\bm{x})\right),~i,j=1,\cdots,K.

Then we have:

\begin{split}\big\|\nabla_{\bm{x}}\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\big\|_{2}=\left(\mathsf{soft}^{2}_{i}(\bm{x})\cdot\sum_{j\neq i}\mathsf{soft}^{2}_{j}(\bm{x})+\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})^{2}\cdot\left(1-\mathsf{soft}_{j}(\bm{x})\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}.\end{split}

Since
\mathsf{soft}^{2}_{i}(\bm{x})\leq\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})
,
\left(1-\mathsf{soft}_{j}(\bm{x})\right)^{2}\leq\left(1-\mathsf{soft}_{j}(\bm{x})\right)
, and
\sum_{j\neq i}\mathsf{soft}^{2}_{j}(\bm{x})\leq(1-\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x}))
, we have:

\big\|\nabla_{\bm{x}}\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\big\|_{2}\leq\bigg(2\cdot\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\cdot\left(1-\mathsf{soft}_{i}(\bm{x})\right)\bigg)^{1/2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},

which ends the proof.

Appendix E
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity and Its Properties

E-A

{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Symmetrization

In this section, we provide the derivation for
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Symmetrization, which is the key technology for Thm.
4
. The main idea is that exchanging instances, but not the terms, does not change the value of

\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)\right)\right]}.

Lemma 8

(
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
symmetrization)
.

The following inequality holds:

\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)\right)\right]}\leq\frac{4\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)},

with the labels
\bm{Y}
fixed for
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

Proof
.

Denote
\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n})=\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}^{\prime}))
and define

\begin{split}T^{i,j,m,n}=&\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}+\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n})+\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}-\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n})\\
&-\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}-\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n})-\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}+\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n}),\end{split}

(26)

we first show that

\begin{split}&\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}\\
&~=\frac{1}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)}\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot T^{i,j,m,n}\right]}.\end{split}

(27)

Given
\mathcal{S}=\left\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{m}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\left\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})\right\}_{i=1}^{m}
, where we sort the instances such that
y_{i}\leq y_{j}
for
i\leq j
for the sake of convenience, since the instances are drawn independently, we employ the fact that:

\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}=\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}}(f)\right)\right]}

(28)

where
\tilde{S}
and
\tilde{S}^{\prime}
are permuted datasets obtained from
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
respectively after exchanging
0\leq x\leq N
samples of
(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})
and
(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{i},y^{\prime}_{i})
sharing the same index.

Next, we show that
for any sequence of i.i.d Rademacher random variables,
\sigma=(\sigma^{(1)}_{1},\cdots,\sigma^{(1)}_{n_{1}},\cdots,\sigma^{(2)}_{1},\cdots,\sigma^{(N_{C})}_{n_{C}})
and any
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, there exists a pair of permuted datasets
\tilde{S}^{\sigma}
and
\tilde{S}^{\prime\sigma}
such that

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot T^{i,j,m,n}\right]=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[R_{\ell,\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(f)-R_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma}}(f).\right]

(29)

Specifically, we show this by induction.

Base Case
. We assume that
\mathcal{S}=\left\{(\bm{x}_{1},1),(\bm{x}_{2},2)\right\}

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\left\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{1},1),(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{2},2)\right\}
,
\sigma=(\sigma^{(1)}_{1},\sigma^{(2)}_{1})
. Here we define

T^{1}=T^{1,2,1,1},T^{2}=T^{2,1,1,1}.

We have the following cases:

(a)

\sigma^{(1)}_{1}=1,\sigma^{(2)}_{1}=1
. We have:

\begin{split}&T^{1}=\ell(f^{(1)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{1},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{2})-\ell(f^{(1)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{1},\bm{\bm{x}}_{2}),\\
&T^{2}=\ell(f^{(2)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{2},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{1})-\ell(f^{(2)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{2},\bm{\bm{x}}_{1}),\\
\end{split}

(30)

This shows that

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[T^{1}+T^{2}\right]=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right].

(31)

This suggests that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{S}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}_{\sigma}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

(b)

\sigma^{(1)}_{1}=1,\sigma^{(2)}_{1}=-1
.

\begin{split}&T^{1}=\ell(f^{(1)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{1},\bm{\bm{x}}_{2})-\ell(f^{(1)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{1},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{2}),\\
&T^{2}=\ell(f^{(2)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{2},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{1})-\ell(f^{(2)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{2},\bm{\bm{x}}_{1}),\\
\end{split}

(32)

This shows that

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[T^{1}+T^{2}\right]=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[R_{\ell,\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(f)-R_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma}}(f)\right].

(33)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma}and\tilde{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}_{\sigma}
are obtained by exchanging
(\bm{x}_{2},2)
and
(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{2},2)

(c)

\sigma^{(1)}_{1}=-1,\sigma^{(2)}_{1}=1
. One can show that the corresponding
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma},\tilde{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}_{\sigma}
are obtained by exchanging
(\bm{x}_{1},1)
and
(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{1},1)
.

(d)

\sigma^{(1)}_{1}=-1,\sigma^{(2)}_{1}=-1
. One can show that the corresponding
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma},\tilde{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}_{\sigma}
are obtained by completely exchanging
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

The arguments above complete the proof for base case.

Recursion
. Given

\mathcal{S}^{-}=\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k},\mathcal{S}^{-^{\prime}}=\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k},\forall\sigma^{-}\in\{-1,1\}^{k},

we assume that
\mathcal{S}^{-},\mathcal{S}^{-^{\prime}}
and
\sigma^{-}
satisfy our conclusion, which is realized by
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{\sigma^{-}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{-}}
. We now show that given a new pair
(\bm{x}_{n},i)
,
(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n},i)
and
\sigma^{(i)}_{n}\in\{-1,1\}
such that

\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{-}\cup\{(\bm{x}_{n},i)\},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{-^{\prime}}\cup\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n},i)\},\sigma=(\sigma^{-},\sigma^{(i)}_{new})

still satisfies our conclusion. Obviously only
T^{i,*,n,*}
and
T^{*,i,*,n}
are involved with the new sample, where
*
simply refers to all the possible choices.

For the case that
\sigma^{(i)}_{n}=-1
, we have the following cases:
First, we examine the terms
T^{i,*,n,*}
by taking any specific
T^{i,j,n,m}
.

(a)

\sigma^{(j)}_{m}=1
, we have:

T^{i,j,n,m}=\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m})-\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m}).

(b)

\sigma^{(j)}_{m}=-1
, we have:

T^{i,j,n,m}=\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m})-\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n}).

Moreover, for any
T^{j,i,n,m}
, we have:

(a)

\sigma^{(j)}_{m}=1
, we have:

T^{j,i,m,n}=\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n})-\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n}).

(b)

\sigma^{(j)}_{m}=-1
, we have:

T^{i,j,n,m}=\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{\bm{x}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}}_{n})-\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{m},\bm{\bm{x}^{\prime}}_{n}).

This shows that

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{\sigma^{-}}\cup\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n},i)\},~\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\sigma}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{-}}\cup\{(\bm{x}_{n},i)\}.

Similarly if
\sigma^{(i)}_{n}=1
, we have

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-}_{\sigma^{-}}\cup\{(\bm{x}_{n},i)\},~\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\sigma}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{-^{\prime}}_{\sigma^{-}}\cup\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n},i)\}.

In either case, we know that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{\sigma}
are obtained by exchanging the corresponding instances of
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.

By the arguments in the base case and the recursion, we complete the proof with:

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot T^{i,j,m,n}\right]=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left[R_{\ell,\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(f)-R_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma}}(f)\right].

(34)

It immediately suggests that

\begin{split}&\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\expe_{\sigma}\bigg[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot T^{i,j,m,n}\bigg]}\\
&~~=\expe_{\sigma}\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(R_{\ell,\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(f)-R_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma}}(f)\right)\right]}\\
&~~=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\cdot\sum_{\sigma}\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(R_{\ell,\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}(f)-R_{\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\sigma}}(f)\right)\right]}\\
&~~=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\cdot\sum_{\sigma}\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}\\
&~~=\frac{2^{N}}{2^{N}}\cdot\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}\\
&~~=\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}.\end{split}

(35)

The proof then follows that

\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot T^{i,j,m,n}\right]}\leq 4\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}).

(36)

ddsadsa

E-B

A General Result

In this section, we will provide a proof for the general result for
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
generalization bound, which is based on Lem.
3
, and Lem.
8
.

Restate of Theorem
4
.

Given dataset
\mathcal{S}=\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
, where the instances are sampled independently, for all multiclass scoring functions
f\in\mathcal{H}
, if
dom\ell=[0,B]
,
\forall\delta\in(0,1)
, the following inequalities hold with probability at least
1-\delta
:

R_{\ell}(f)\leq\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+\frac{4\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}+\frac{10B}{N_{C}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}{2N}}

where
\xi(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n_{C}}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho_{i}}}}.

Proof
.

Given
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
as another dataset being independent with
\mathcal{S}
, since
\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)
, using the Jensen’s inequality, we have:

\begin{split}\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)}-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)\right)\right]}&=\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\expe_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}{\left[\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)\right]}}\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)\right)\right]}\end{split}

From Lem.
8
, we have:

\begin{split}\expe_{\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S^{\prime}}}{\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\left(\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}(f)\right)\right]}\leq 4\frac{1}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)}\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}).\end{split}

(37)

Given
\mathcal{S}
, define
\mathcal{S}_{m}=(\mathcal{S}~\backslash~\{(\bm{x}_{m},y_{m})\})\cup{\{(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{m},y_{m})\}}
.
We now proceed to derive the bounded difference property for the loss function. Suppose that
y_{m}=i
, we have:

\begin{split}d_{i}&=|\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}(\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)}-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f))-\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}{\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(f)}-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(f))|\\
&\leq\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}|\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)-\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}(f)|\\
&=\nicefrac{{1}}{{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}}\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\bigg[\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}|\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{x}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})-\ell(f^{(i)},\bm{x}^{\prime}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})|\\
&~~~~+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}|\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{m})-\ell(f^{(j)},\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}^{\prime}_{m})|\bigg]\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \frac{2B}{N_{C}{n}_{i}}\end{split}

Thus we have
\sum_{i=1}^{N}d_{i}^{2}=4\nicefrac{{B^{2}}}{{N_{C}^{2}}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n_{C}}\nicefrac{{1}}{{n_{i}}}
.
According to Eq.(
37
) and Lem.
3
, we know that fixing the labels
\bm{Y}
, with probability at least
1-\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
, the following inequality holds:

\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)}\leq\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+\frac{4}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})+\frac{2B}{N_{C}}\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}{2N}}

(38)

Using an analogous argument, we can prove that, fixing the labels
\bm{Y}
, with probability at least
1-\nicefrac{{\delta}}{{2}}
, the following inequality holds:

\frac{\mathfrak{R}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\leq\frac{\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}+\frac{2B}{N_{C}}\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}{2N}}.

(39)

Combining Eq.(
38
) and Eq.(
39
), fixing
\bm{Y}
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that:

\expe_{\mathcal{S}}{\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)}\leq\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+\frac{4}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})+\frac{10B}{N_{C}}\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}{2N}}

(40)

Following the arguments in Thm.8 in
[
2
]
, we have Eq.(
40
) holds with probability (over
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
) at least
1-\delta
.

E-C

Sub-Gaussian Property of the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity

Definition 6

(Sub-Gaussian Stochastic Process)
.

A stochastic process
\theta\mapsto X_{\theta}
with indexing set
\mathcal{T}
is sub-Gaussian to a pseudo-metric
d
on
\mathcal{T}
, if for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\expe\left[\exp\left(\lambda\cdot(X_{\theta}-X_{\theta^{\prime}})\right)\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}d(\theta,\theta^{\prime})^{2}}{2}\right).

(41)

Define
T_{f}(\sigma)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}T^{i,j,m,n}
, and recall that

T^{i,j,m,n}=\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}+\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))}{n_{i}n_{j}},

with the function
f
chosen from an index set (in this case a function class)
\mathcal{F}
. We see that
{T_{f}(\sigma)}_{f\in\mathcal{F}}
is essentially a stochastic process of the Rademacher random variables
\sigma
, which is revealed by the following lemma:

Lemma 9
.

Given an input feature set
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}=\{\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{N}\}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}
is defined as

\big\{(\bm{x},y):\bm{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}},y\in\{1,2,\cdots,N_{C}\}\big\},

which is the cartesian product between
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
and the label space. Moreover,
\forall\bm{z}=(\bm{x},i)\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}
,
s(\bm{z})=s^{(i)}(\bm{x})
.
With the notations above, if
\ell
is
\phi_{\ell}
-Lipschitz continuous, we have that,
\{C_{G}\cdot T_{f}(\sigma)\}_{f\in\mathcal{H}}
with
f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{N_{C}})
and
f^{(i)}=\mathsf{soft}^{(i)}\circ\bm{s}
is a sub-Gaussian stochastic process in the sense that :

\expe_{\sigma}\Bigg[\exp\bigg(\lambda\cdot C_{G}\cdot\big(T_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})\big)\bigg)\Bigg]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}d_{\infty}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}})^{2}}{2}\right).

(42)

where

C_{G}=\dfrac{1}{\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}},~~~d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}(\bm{s},\tilde{\bm{s}})=\max_{\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}}|\bm{s}(\bm{z})-\tilde{\bm{s}}({\bm{z}})|.

(43)

Proof
.

Denote
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}
as a set of Rademacher random variables in the following form:

{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma}}=(\sigma^{(1)}_{1},\cdots,\sigma^{(1)}_{n_{1}},\cdots,\sigma^{(i)}_{1},\cdots,{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma^{(i)}_{k}},\cdots,\sigma^{(i)}_{n_{1}},\cdots,\sigma^{(N_{C})}_{n_{N_{C}}})

and denote
{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\sigma_{\backslash(i,k)}}
as another set of Rademacher random variables with all entries of which the same as
\sigma
except that
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma^{(i)}_{k}
is replaced with
\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{k}
, i.e,

{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\sigma_{\backslash(i,k)}}=(\sigma^{(1)}_{1},\cdots,\sigma^{(1)}_{n_{1}},\cdots,\sigma^{(i)}_{1},\cdots,{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{k}},\cdots,\sigma^{(i)}_{n_{1}},\cdots,\sigma^{(N_{C})}_{n_{N_{C}}}).

We have the following bounded difference property for every possible choice of
(i,k)
:

\begin{split}diff_{i}&=\left|\left(T_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})\right)-\left(T_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\sigma_{\backslash(i,k)}})-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\sigma_{\backslash(i,k)}})\right)\right|\\
&=\bigg|\left(\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\sigma^{(i)}_{k}}}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\tilde{\sigma}^{(i)}_{k}}}{2}\right)\cdot\bigg[\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\left(\ell({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}^{(i)},\bm{x}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})-\ell({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}^{(i)},\bm{x}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})\right)\\
&~~~~+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\left(\ell({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}^{(j)},\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{m})-\ell({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}^{(j)},\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{m})\right)\bigg]\bigg|\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \frac{2\cdot(N_{C}-1)}{{n}_{i}}\max_{i,~\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i},~\bm{x}_{n}\notin\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left|\ell({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}^{(i)},\bm{x}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})-\ell({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}^{(i)},\bm{x}_{m},\bm{x}_{n})\right|\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \frac{4\cdot(N_{C}-1)}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\max_{i,~\bm{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}}\left|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}^{(i)}(\bm{x})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}^{(i)}(\bm{x})\right|\\
&=\ \ \ \ \frac{4\cdot(N_{C}-1)}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\max_{i,~\bm{x}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\left<\nabla\mathsf{soft}^{(i)}\left(\tau\cdot\bm{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}}(\bm{x})+(1-\tau)\cdot{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}(\bm{x})\right),~\left({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}(\bm{x})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}(\bm{x})\right)\right>d\tau\right|\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \frac{4\cdot(N_{C}-1)}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\left(\sup_{\bm{x}}||\nabla\mathsf{soft}^{(i)}(\bm{x})||_{1}\right)\cdot\max_{\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}(\bm{z})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}(\bm{z})|\\
&\leq\ \ \ \ \frac{2\cdot(N_{C}-1)}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\max_{\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}(\bm{z})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}(\bm{z})|.\end{split}

It is now easy to see that
C_{G}\cdot\big(T_{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\sigma})\big)
satisfies the bounded difference property (Def.
5
). According to Lem.
1
, we could choose
v
therein as:

v=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{G}^{2}\cdot diff_{i}^{2}=\max_{\bm{z}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}}|\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s}}(\bm{z})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}(\bm{z})|^{2}

This suffices to complete the proof based on Lem.
1
.
∎

E-D

Chaining Bounds

Now with the sub-Gaussian property proved in Lem.
9
, we can derive chaining upper bounds for the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher complexity based on the notion of covering number. Before presenting the results, let us start with the definition of
\epsilon
-covering and the covering number.

Restate of Definition
3

(
\epsilon
-covering)
.

[
39
]
Let
(\mathcal{H},d)
be a (pseudo)metric space, and
\Theta\in\mathcal{H}
.
\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{K}\}
is said to be an
\epsilon
-covering of
\Theta
if
\Theta\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal{B}(h_{i},\epsilon)
,
i.e.
,
\forall\theta\in\Theta
,
\exists i~s.t.~d(\theta,h_{i})\leq\epsilon
.

Restate of Definition
4

(Covering Number)
.

[
39
]
Based on the notations in Def.
3
, the covering number of
\Theta
with radius
\epsilon
is defined as:

\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\Theta,d)=\min\left\{n:\exists\epsilon-\text{covering over $\Theta$ with size n}\right\}.

Restate of Theorem
5

(Chaining Bounds for
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity)
.

Suppose that the score function
s^{(i)}
maps
\mathcal{X}
onto a bounded interval
[-R_{s},R_{s}]
, the following properties hold for
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})
:

(a)

For a decreasing precision sequence
\{\epsilon_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
, with
\epsilon_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{k},~k=1,2,\cdots,K-1
and
\epsilon_{0}\geq R_{s}
, we have:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})\leq N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K}+6\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{K}\epsilon_{k}\phi_{\ell}(N_{C}-1)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}\end{split}

(b)

There exists a universal constant
C
, such that:

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})\leq C\cdot\inf_{R_{s}\geq\alpha\geq 0}\left(N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\alpha+\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\int_{\alpha}^{R_{s}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}d\epsilon\right)

Proof
.

First of all, we have:

\begin{split}2C_{G}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})=&C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}\in\mathcal{H}}T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)+\sup_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right]\\
&=C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}\in\mathcal{H}}T_{f}(\sigma)+\sup_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(-\sigma)\right]\\
&=C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}\in\mathcal{H}}T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)+\sup_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right]\\
&=C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\end{split}

(44)

Now define
\hat{\mathcal{H}}
as an
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
with the pseudo-metric
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}
. Choose
{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}},{{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}
, such that
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}},{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}})\leq\epsilon
and
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}},{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}})\leq\epsilon
. We then have the following result:

\begin{split}&C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\\
&=C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)\right)+\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}(\sigma)\right)+\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\\
&\leq 2C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{d_{\infty,\mathcal{N}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}}})\leq\epsilon}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]+C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}}}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\end{split}

(45)

Furthermore, let
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
be an
\epsilon_{k}
-cover of
\mathcal{H}
. For each
k
, pick
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}_{k}}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{k}}}
such that
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}_{k}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}})\leq\epsilon_{k}
and
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}({{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{k}}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}})\leq\epsilon_{k}
. Specifically, we take
\epsilon_{K}=\epsilon
,
\epsilon_{0}\geq R_{s}
, which allows us to choose
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}_{0}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{0}}}
,
\epsilon_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{k}
,
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{K}}}={{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}
, and
{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{K}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}
. With
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{k}}}=\mathsf{soft}\circ{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}_{k}}}
and
{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{k}}}=\mathsf{soft}\circ{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{k}}}
. Then, for
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}
, we can decompose
T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)
as

T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)=T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{K}}}}(\sigma)=T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{0}}}}(\sigma)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{k}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}_{k-1}}}}(\sigma)\right)~~~\text{and}~~~T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}(\sigma)=T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{K}}}}(\sigma)=T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{0}}}}(\sigma)+\sum_{i=1}^{K}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{k}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}_{k-1}}}}(\sigma)\right).

Based on the decompositions, we have:

\begin{split}C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{\bm{s}}}}}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\epsilon}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\hat{\tilde{f}}}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\leq 2\cdot C_{G}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{K}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}\hat{\bm{s}}_{k}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k},\hat{\bm{s}}_{k-1}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k-1}\\
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}(\hat{\bm{s}}_{k},\hat{\bm{s}}_{k-1})\leq 3\epsilon_{k}\end{subarray}}\left(T_{\hat{f}_{k}}(\sigma)-T_{\hat{f}_{k-1}}(\sigma)\right)\right].\end{split}

(46)

According to maximal inequality (Lem.
D-A2
), we have:

C_{G}\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}\hat{\bm{s}}_{k}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k},\hat{\bm{s}}_{k-1}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k-1}\\
d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}(\hat{\bm{s}}_{k},\hat{\bm{s}}_{k-1})\leq 3\epsilon_{k}\end{subarray}}\left(T_{\hat{f}_{k}}(\sigma)-T_{\hat{f}_{k-1}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\leq 3\epsilon_{k}\sqrt{2\log{|\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k}|\cdot|\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{k-1}|}}\leq 6\epsilon_{k}\sqrt{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}.

(47)

Following a similar derivation to Lem.
9
, we have:

\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{d_{\infty,\mathcal{N}}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{s}}},{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{\bm{s}}}})\leq\epsilon_{K}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\hat{f}}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\leq N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K}

(48)

Putting all together, we have:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})&=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{H}}\left(T_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}f}}}(\sigma)-T_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{f}}}(\sigma)\right)\right]\leq N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K}+\left(\frac{1}{C_{G}}\right)6\sum_{i=1}\epsilon_{k}\sqrt{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}\\
&\leq N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K}+6\sum_{i=1}^{K}\epsilon_{k}\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}\end{split}

This ends the proof of (a).

Given the result of (a), we now turn to proof (b). First note that
\epsilon_{k}=2(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k+1})
and that
\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}})
is non-increasing with respect to
\epsilon
.
We then have:

\begin{split}&N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K}+6\sum_{i=1}^{K}\epsilon_{k}\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}\\
&\leq 2N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\epsilon_{K+1}+12\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k+1})\cdot(N_{C}-1)\phi_{\ell}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}\end{split}

Furthermore, we have:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})&\leq 12\phi_{\ell}\left(N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{K+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\epsilon_{k}-\epsilon_{k+1})\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon_{k},\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}\right)\\
&\leq 12\phi_{\ell}\bigg(N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{K+1}+\int_{\epsilon_{K+1}}^{R_{s}}(N_{C}-1)\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}d\epsilon\\
&~~~+\int_{R_{s}}^{\epsilon_{0}}(N_{C}-1)\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}d\epsilon\bigg)\\
&=12\phi_{\ell}\left(N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\epsilon_{K+1}+\int_{\epsilon_{K+1}}^{R_{s}}\xi(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}}))}{N}}d\epsilon\right)\end{split}

It then becomes clear that the universal constant should be chosen as
C=12
. Set
\alpha=\epsilon_{K+1}
. By proper choices of
K
and
\epsilon_{0}
and let
\alpha=\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2^{K+1}}
, we see that the inequality holds for all
\alpha\in[0,R_{s}]
, which thus completes the proof.
∎

According to Thm.
5
, we have the following theorem as a result of a new minorization technique which appears in a recent work
[
65
]
.

Restate of Theorem
6

(Transformation Upper Bound)
.

Given the Hypothesis class

\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F}=\bigg\{\bm{g}(\bm{x})=\mathsf{soft}(\bm{s}(\bm{x})):~\bm{s}\in\mathcal{F}\bigg\},

where
\mathsf{soft}(\cdot)
is the softmax function. Suppose that
\bm{s}(x)\subseteq[-R_{s},R_{s}]^{N_{C}}
and
\ell
is
\phi_{\ell}
-Lipschitz continuous, the following inequality holds:

\begin{split}&\frac{\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F})}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\leq\phi_{\ell}\bigg(2^{9}\cdot\frac{1}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{N_{C}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\log^{3/2}\left(e\cdot R_{s}\cdot N\cdot N_{C}\right)\cdot\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}(\Pi\circ\mathcal{F})+\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}\bigg),\end{split}

(49)

where the Rademacher complexity
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}(\Pi\circ\mathcal{F})
is defined as:

\begin{split}&\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}(\Pi\circ\mathcal{F})\\
&~~=\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(N_{C})})\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{1}{N\cdot N_{C}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma^{(i)}_{j}\cdot f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{i})\right],\end{split}

(50)

where
\{\sigma^{(i)}_{j}\}_{(i,j)}
is a sequence of independent Rademacher random variables.

Proof
.

With the help of the chaining bound in Thm.
5
-a)
, the proof follows an analogous spirit as
[
65
,
Prop.1]
, with the parameters therein set as
\lambda=1,~\theta=0,~q=N_{C},~n=N.

The result in Thm.
6
directly relates the complicated pairwise Rademacher complexity
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F})
to the instance-wise Rademacher complexity
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}
. This makes the derivation of the generalization upper bound much easier. Once a bound on the ordinary Rademacher complexity over the functional class
\mathcal{F}
is available, we can directly plug it in to this theorem and find a resulting bound over the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
complexity.

Appendix F
Practical Generalization Bounds

F-A

Practical Results for Linear Models

In this section, we provide concrete generalization bounds for two practical models.

Restate of Theorem
7
.

Based on the assumptions of Thm.
4
, we have the Given the hypothesis space for
\ell_{p}
norm penalized linear model as:

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma}=\big\{&f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(n_{C})}):f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\bm{W}^{(i)}\bm{x},\\
&||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma\big\},\end{split}

if
\ell
is
\phi_{\ell}
-Lipschitz continuous, and the input features are sampled from
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathcal{R}^{d}
, and for all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have
||\bm{x}||^{2}_{2}\leq R
with
0<p<\infty
,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{\bar{p}}=1
, for all
f\in\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma}
, we have the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

{R}_{\ell}(f)\leq\hat{R}_{\ell,\mathcal{S}}(f)+\mathcal{I}_{{Lin}}\bigg(\chi(\bm{Y}),\xi({\bm{Y}}),\delta\bigg)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}},

where

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}_{{Lin}}\bigg(\chi(\bm{Y}),\xi({\bm{Y}}),\delta\bigg)=\frac{4R_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{\ell}\gamma}{N_{C}-1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2(\bar{p}-1)}{N_{C}}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})\\
&+\dfrac{5B}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{2\log\left(\dfrac{2}{\delta}\right)}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y}).\end{split}

Proof
.

Follows Thm.
4
, Lem.
10

F-B

Practical Results for Deep Fully-connected Neural Networks

Settings
. We denote an
L
-layer deep fully-connected neural network with
N_{C}
-way output as :

f(\bm{x})=\bm{W}f_{\bm{\omega},L}(\bm{x})=\bm{W}s(\bm{\omega}_{L-2}\cdots s(\bm{\omega}_{1}\bm{x})),

The notations are as follows:
s(\cdot)
is the activation function;
n_{h_{j}}
is the number of hidden neurons for the
j
-th layer;
\bm{\omega}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{h_{j+1}}\times n_{h_{j}}},j=1,2,\cdots,L-2
are the weights for the first
L-1
layers;
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{h_{L-1}}\times N_{C}}
is the weight for the output layer. Moreover, the
i
-th output of the network is defined as :

f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}f_{\bm{\omega},L}(\bm{x}).

where
\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}
is the i-th row of
\bm{W}
. In the next theorem, we focus on a specific hypothesis class for such networks where the product of weight norms

\Pi_{\bm{W},\bm{\omega}}=||\bm{W}||_{F}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{L-2}||\bm{\omega}_{j}||_{F}

are no more than
\gamma
. We denote such a hypothesis class as
\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}=\bigg\{&f:f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}f_{\bm{\omega},L}(\bm{x}),||f^{(i)}||_{\infty}\leq R_{s},~i=1,\cdots,N_{C},~\Pi_{\bm{W},\bm{\omega}}\leq\gamma\bigg\},\end{split}

To obtain the final output, we perform a softmax operation over
f(\bm{x})
. Thus, a valid model under this setting could be chosen from the following hypothesis class:

\begin{split}\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}=\bigg\{g:&g^{(i)}=\dfrac{\exp(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}))}{\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\exp(f^{(j)}(\bm{x}))},~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}\bigg\}.\end{split}

Restate of Theorem
8

(
Practical Generalization Bounds for Deep Models
)
.

Based on the assumptions of Thm.
4

where
s(\cdot)
is a 1-Lipshiptz and positive homogeneous activation function. If
\ell
is
\phi_{\ell}
-Lipschitz continuous, and the input features are sampled from
\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and for all
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have
||\bm{x}||^{2}_{2}\leq R_{\mathcal{X}}
, for all
f\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}
, we have the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

R_{\ell}(f)\leq\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+\min\bigg(\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,1}},~\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,2}}\bigg)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}

where
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}}},~~\xi(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho_{i}}}},~~\rho_{i}=\dfrac{{n}_{i}}{N},

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,1}}=C_{1}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\frac{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}-1}+\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{1}R_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{\ell}\gamma}{2}\cdot C_{3}+\dfrac{C_{2}B}{{N_{C}}}\cdot\sqrt{2\log\left(\dfrac{2}{\delta}\right)}\bigg)\cdot\xi(\bm{Y}),\end{split}

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,2}}=&C_{1}\phi_{\ell}\bigg(\frac{2^{9}}{N_{C}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\log^{3/2}\left(K\cdot N\cdot N_{C}\right)\gamma\cdot R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot(\sqrt{2\log(2)L}+1)+1\bigg)+C_{2}\frac{B\cdot\sqrt{{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\end{split}

C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are universal constants as thm.
4
,
K=e\cdot R_{s}
,
C_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{L\log 2}+\sqrt{N_{C}}}{\sqrt{N_{C}-1}}
.

Proof
.

Follows Thm.
4
, Lem.
11
, Thm.
6
and the fact that
\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}(\Pi\circ\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{R_{\mathcal{X}}\gamma(\sqrt{2\log(2)L}+1)}{N\cdot N_{C}}
from Thm.1 in
[
27
]
.

F-C

Practical Results for Deep Convolutional Neural Networks

Now we use the result in Thm.
5
to derive a generalization bound for a general class of deep neural networks where fully-connected layers and convolutional layers coexist. In a nutshell, the result is essentially an application of Thm.
5
to a recent idea appeared in
[
49
]
.

General Setting.
Now we are ready to introduce the setting of the deep neural networks employed in the forthcoming theoretical analysis, which is adopted from
[
49
]
. We focus on the deep neural networks with
N_{conn}
fully-connected layers and
N_{conv}
convolutional layers. The
i
-th convolutional layer has a kernel
\bm{K}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{i}\times k_{i}\times c_{i-1}\times c_{i}}
. Recall that convolution is a linear operator. For a Given kernel
\bm{K}
, we denote its associated matrix as
op(\bm{K})
, such that
\bm{K}(\bm{x})=op(\bm{K})\bm{x}
. Moreover, we assume that, each time, the convolution layer is followed by a componentwise non-linear activation function and an optional pooling operation. We assume that the activation functions and the pooling operations are all
1
-Lipschitz. For the
i
-th fully-connected layer, we denote its weight as
\bm{V}^{(i)}
. Above all, the complete parameter set of a given deep neural network could be represented as
\bm{P}=\{\bm{K}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{K}^{(N_{conv})},\bm{V}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{V}^{(N_{conn})}\}
. Again, we also assume that the loss function is
\phi_{\ell}
-Lipschitz and
Range\{\ell\}\subseteq[0,B]
. Finally, given two deep neural networks with parameters
{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}
, we adopt a metric
d_{NN}(\cdot,\cdot)
to measure their distance:

d_{NN}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}})=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{conv}}||op({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{K}^{(i)}}})-op({\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{K}}^{(i)}})||_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{conn}}||{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{V}^{(i)}}}-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{V}}^{(i)}}||_{2}

Constraints Over the Parameters
. First, we define
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}_{\nu}
as the class for
initialization of the parameters
:

\mathcal{P}^{(0)}_{\nu}=\bigg\{\bm{P}:\left(\max_{i\in\{1,\cdots,N_{conv}\}}||op(\bm{K}^{(i)})||_{2}\right)\leq 1+\nu,~\left(\max_{j\in\{1,\cdots,N_{conn}\}}||\bm{V}^{(j)}||_{2}\right)\leq 1+\nu\bigg\}

Now we further assume that the learned parameters should be chosen from a class denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{\beta,\nu}
, where the distance between the learned parameter and the fixed initialization residing in
\mathcal{P}^{(0)}_{\nu}
is no bigger than
\beta
:

\mathcal{P}_{\beta,\nu}=\bigg\{\bm{P}:d_{NN}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}_{0})\leq\beta,~{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}^{(0)}_{\nu}\bigg\}.

The following result then provides a generalization upper bound based on Thm.
4
and Lem.
13
.

Restate of Theorem
9
.

Based on the setting in Lem.
13
, given dataset
\mathcal{S}=\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
, where the instances are sampled independently, for all multiclass scoring functions
f\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu}
, if
Range\{\ell\}\subseteq[0,B]
,
\forall\delta\in(0,1)
, the following inequalities hold with probability at least
1-\delta
:

\begin{split}R_{\ell}(f)\leq&\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+C_{1}\cdot\left(\frac{\tilde{C}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot R_{s}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log\left(3{\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}{N}}\right)\right)}{{N}}}\right)+C_{2}\cdot\frac{B\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}{{N}}}\end{split}

where
C_{1},C_{2},\tilde{C}
are universal constants.

F-D

Essential Lemmas

F-D
1
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity for p-norm penalized Linear Models

Lemma 10

(
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity for Linear Model)
.

We have:

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma})\leq R_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{\ell}\gamma\cdot\sqrt{2(\bar{p}-1)\cdot N_{C}}\cdot{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}\\

(51)

where

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}}},~~\rho_{i}=\frac{{n}_{i}}{N}.

Proof
.

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma})&=\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{H}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}+\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right]\\
\\
&=\expe_{\sigma}\left[\sup_{\bm{W},||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}+\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\ell(\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
\\
&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}^{(i)}\\
||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma\end{subarray}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\ell(\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
\\
&~~+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}^{(i)}\\
||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma\end{subarray}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\ell(\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma})&\overset{(*)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}^{(i)}\\
||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma\end{subarray}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right]\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{W}^{(i)}\\
||\bm{W}^{(i)}||_{p}\leq\gamma\end{subarray}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\bm{W}^{(i)^{\top}}(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right]\end{split}

That is to say,

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma})&{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{\bar{p}}\right]\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{\bar{p}}\right]\\
\\
&\overset{(**)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\bigg(\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{\bar{p}}^{\bar{p}}\right]\bigg)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\bar{p}}}}\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\bigg(\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{\bar{p}}^{\bar{p}}\bigg)\right]^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\bar{p}}}},\\
\\
\end{split}

(52)

where
(*)
is due to the Talagrand contraction lemma,
(**)
follows that fact that
()^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\bar{p}}}}
is concave for
\bar{p}>1
and Jensen’s Inequality.

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H}^{Lin}_{p,\gamma})&\overset{(***)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sqrt{\bar{p}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\cdot\sqrt{\bar{p}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\\
\\
&\overset{(****)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\gamma\sqrt{\bar{p}-1}\sqrt{N\cdot N_{C}}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\\
\\
&~~~~~+\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\bigg)^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\\
\\
&\leq R_{\mathcal{X}}\phi_{\ell}\gamma\sqrt{2\frac{\bar{p}-1}{N}}\cdot\sqrt{N_{C}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\cdot\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\rho_{i}\rho_{j}}},\end{split}

where
(***)
is due to Lem.
4
, and
(****)
is due to
\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

\|\bm{x}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{N}\|\bm{x}\|_{2}
, and the last inequality follows that :

\begin{split}&\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{1}{4n_{i}^{2}n_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{R_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}{n_{i}^{2}n_{j}^{2}}\\
&\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{4n_{i}^{2}n_{j}^{2}}\|\bm{x}_{m}-\bm{x}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{R_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}{n_{i}^{2}n_{j}^{2}}\end{split}

F-D
2
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity for A class of Deep Fully-connected Neural Networks

Lemma 11
.

we have:
