\begin{split}&\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma})\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\phi_{\ell}R_{\mathcal{X}}\gamma N_{C}\xi(\bm{Y})\bigg(\sqrt{\frac{L\log 2(N_{C}-1)}{N}}+\sqrt{\frac{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}{N}}\bigg)\\
&~~+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\phi_{\ell}N_{C}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}\end{split}

where
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}}},\xi(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{\rho_{i}}},~~\rho_{i}=\frac{{n}_{i}}{N}.

Proof
.

Similar to Lem.
10
, we can achieve the bound:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma})&\leq\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
\\
\end{split}

By the sub-additivity of supremum, we have:

\begin{split}\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma})&\leq\underbrace{\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}(N_{C}-1)\cdot\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{i}}\cdot(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\right]}_{(a)}\\
\\
&~~+\underbrace{\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{j}}\cdot(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right]}_{(b)}\\
\\
&~~+\underbrace{\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right]}_{(c)}\\
\\
&~~+\underbrace{\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\sup_{g\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN,nh}_{\gamma}}\left[(g^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\cdot\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right]}_{(d)}\end{split}

(53)

We first derive the bound for
(a)+(b)
.

According Lem.
5
and Lem.
7
, we know that:

\begin{split}(a)+(b)\leq\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}(N_{C}-1)\cdot\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\bigg[&\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{i}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\bigg]\\
\\
&~~+\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{j}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\end{split}

Using the Lem.
6
recursively towards hierarchical structure of DNN, we have:

\begin{split}&\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\right]\\
&~~~~~~\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\exp\left(\lambda\cdot\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\right]\right)\right)\\
&~~~~~~\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\left(\left(\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\exp\left(\lambda\cdot\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\right)\right]\right)\right)\\
&~~~~~~\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\bigg(2^{L}\cdot\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\gamma\left\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\bm{x}_{m}\right\|\right)\right]\bigg)\\
\end{split}

\begin{split}&\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot(f^{(c)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right]\\
&~~~~~~\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\bigg(2^{L}\cdot\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\exp\left(\lambda\gamma\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\bm{x}_{n}\right\|\right)\right]\bigg).\end{split}

This suggests that:

\begin{split}(a+b)\leq\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\bigg(2^{L}\cdot\expe_{\sigma}\bigg[\exp\bigg(\lambda\gamma&(N_{C}-1)\bigg\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\bm{x}_{m}\bigg\|\\
&+\lambda\gamma\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{j}}\cdot\bm{x}_{n}\right\|\bigg)\bigg]\bigg),\end{split}

which is due to the property that
\expe_{\bm{a},\bm{b}}(f(\bm{a})\cdot g(\bm{a}))=\expe_{\bm{a}}(f(\bm{a}))\cdot\expe_{\bm{b}}(g(\bm{b}))
, if
\bm{a}
and
\bm{b}
are independent.
Denote

\begin{split}\bm{Z}_{i}=\gamma(N_{C}-1)\bigg\|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}&\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{i}}\cdot\bm{x}_{m}\bigg\|+\gamma\left\|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{{n}_{j}}\cdot\bm{x}_{n}\right\|\end{split}

(54)

as a random variable with respect to the Rademacher random variables. We have:

\begin{split}(a)+(b)&\leq\phi_{\ell}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{L\log 2+\log(\expe_{\sigma}\exp(\lambda(\bm{Z}_{i}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}_{i})))}{\lambda}+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\expe_{\sigma}(\bm{Z}_{i})\right).\\
\end{split}

Similar to the proof of Lem.
10
, we can bound

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\expe_{\sigma}(\bm{Z}_{i})\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\gamma\cdot R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot(N_{C})^{3/2}\left((N_{C}-1)\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\cdot\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\rho_{i}}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

(55)

Utilizing the bounded difference inequality (Lem.
1
) of
\bm{Z}_{i}
, we come to the following result:

\expe_{\sigma}\exp\left(\lambda(\bm{Z}_{i}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}_{i})\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}v_{i}}{2}\right),~~v_{i}\leq\frac{N_{C}}{4}\frac{(N_{C}-1)^{2}}{{n}_{i}}\gamma^{2}R_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}+\frac{N_{C}}{4}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\gamma^{2}R_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}}{{n}_{j}},

(56)

which leads to the bound:

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{L\log 2+\log(\expe_{\sigma}\exp(\lambda(\bm{Z}_{i}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}_{i})))}{\lambda}\right)\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{L\log 2+(\dfrac{\lambda^{2}v_{i}}{2})}{\lambda}\right)

(57)

By choosing
\lambda=\sqrt{\frac{2N_{C}L\log 2}{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}v_{i}}}
, we reach the optimal bound as:

\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{L\log 2+\log(\expe_{\sigma}\exp(\lambda(\bm{Z}_{i}-\mathbb{E}(\bm{Z}_{i})))}{\lambda}&\\
\\
&~~~\leq\sqrt{{2N_{C}L\log 2}\cdot{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}v_{i}}}\\
&~~~~=R\cdot\gamma\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\log 2}{2N}}\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{N_{C}-1}{\rho_{i}}}.\end{split}

(58)

Putting all together, we have

\begin{split}(a)+(b)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\phi_{\ell}R_{\mathcal{X}}\gamma\cdot(N_{C}(N_{C}-1))^{1/2}\xi(\bm{Y})\left(\sqrt{\frac{N_{C}L\log 2}{N}}+N_{C}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}\right)\end{split}

(59)

For (c) and (d), we have:

\begin{split}(c)&\leq\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left(\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}\left|f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right|\right)\cdot\left|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right|\right]\\
\\
&\overset{(*)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right|\right]\end{split}

(60)

Similarly, we have :

\begin{split}(d)\overset{(**)}{\leq}\phi_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\sigma^{(j)}_{m}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right|\right],\end{split}

(61)

where (*) and (**) are due to the fact that the softmax function is upper bounded by 1, i.e.,

\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}|f^{(i)}_{W,L}(\bm{x}_{n})|\leq 1~~\text{and}~~\sup_{~~~f\in\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}}|f^{(i)}_{W,L}(\bm{x}_{m})|\leq 1.

This finishes the proof for Eq.(
60
) and Eq.(
61
).

Now we can reach that:

\begin{split}(c)+(d)&\leq\phi_{\ell}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left|\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right|\right]\\
\\
&~~~~+\phi_{\ell}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left|\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right|\right]\\
\\
&\leq\phi_{\ell}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sqrt{\expe_{\sigma^{(i)}}\left[\left(\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{m,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right)^{2}\right]}\\
\\
&~~~~+\phi_{\ell}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sqrt{\expe_{\sigma^{(j)}}\left[\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\sum_{c=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{\sigma^{(i)}_{n,c}}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right)^{2}\right]}\end{split}

(62)

Similar to the proof of
10
, we have:

(c)+(d)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\chi$}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\chi$}}}(\bm{Y})\cdot N_{C}\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}

(63)

Now we provide an alternative upper bound by the chaining technique.

Lemma 12
.

Under the setting of Thm.
8
.

For all
f\in\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{H}^{DNN}_{\gamma,R_{s},n_{h}}
, we have the following inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta
:

R_{\ell}(f)\leq\hat{R}_{\mathcal{S}}(f)+\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}}

where
\xi(\bm{Y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\nicefrac{{1}}{{\rho_{i}}}},~~\rho_{i}=\dfrac{{n}_{i}}{N},

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{{DNN,2}}=&C_{1}\phi_{\ell}\bigg(\frac{2^{9}}{N_{C}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\log^{3/2}\left(K\cdot N\cdot N_{C}\right)\cdot\gamma\cdot R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot(\sqrt{2\log(2)L}+1)+1\bigg)+C_{2}\frac{B\cdot\sqrt{{\log(\nicefrac{{2}}{{\delta}})}}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\end{split}

C_{1}
,
C_{2}
are universal constants as thm.
4
,
K=e\cdot R_{s}
.

Proof
.

The result follows from Thm.
6
, and the fact that

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{N\cdot N_{C}}(\Pi\circ\mathcal{F})\leq\frac{R_{\mathcal{X}}\gamma\cdot(\sqrt{2\log(2)L}+1)}{\sqrt{N_{C}\cdot N}},

which follows from Thm.1 in
[
27
]

F-D
3
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
Rademacher Complexity for A Class of Deep Convolutional Neural Networks

Lemma 13
.

Denote the hypothesis class,

\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu}=\bigg\{\bm{g}(\bm{x})=\mathsf{soft}(\bm{s}_{\bm{P}}(\bm{x})):~\bm{s}_{\bm{P}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu}\bigg\},

\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu}=\{s_{\bm{P}}:\mathbb{R}^{N_{N_{L}-1}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N_{C}}|~\bm{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\beta,\nu},Range(\bm{s}_{P})\subseteq[-R_{s},R_{s}]^{N_{C}}\}

Moreover, define
\tilde{N}=\dfrac{1}{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}}}
we assume that
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}||vec(\bm{x})||\leq R_{\mathcal{X}}
and

R_{s}>1/\min\left\{\sqrt{{N}},\frac{\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log(3R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\beta\cdot{N})\right)}\right\},

we have:

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu})\leq\tilde{C}\left(\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot R_{s}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log\left(3{\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}{N}}\right)\right)}{{N}}}\right),

(64)

where
N_{L}=N_{conv}+N_{conn}
,
N_{par}
is total number of parameters in the neural network, and
\tilde{C}
is a universal constant.

Proof
.

From
[
49
,
Lem.3.4]
, we know that:

\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{D}}}|\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}}}(\bm{z})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}}(\bm{z})|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}||\bm{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}s}}_{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}}}(\bm{x})-{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{s}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}}(\bm{x})||\leq R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\beta\cdot\left(1+\nu+\frac{\beta}{N_{L}}\right)^{N_{L}}\cdot d_{NN}({{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bm{P}}},{\color[rgb]{0.207,0,0.8281}\tilde{\bm{P}}}).

Furthermore, according to
[
49
,
Lem.A.8]
, we have:

\log(\mathfrak{C}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu},d_{\infty,\mathcal{S}})\leq N_{par}\cdot\log\left(\frac{3C_{L}}{\epsilon}\right),

where
C_{L}=R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\beta\exp(\nu N_{L}+\beta)
. Following Thm.
5
-b)
, we have:

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathcal{H})\leq C\cdot\inf_{R_{s}\geq\alpha\geq 0}\left(N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\phi_{\ell}\cdot\alpha+\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot\int_{\alpha}^{R_{s}}\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log(3\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}/\epsilon)\right)}{\tilde{N}}}d\epsilon\right)

Since
R_{s}>1/\sqrt{N}
, we can choose
\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{1}{{N}}}
, the result follows from the inequality that:

\int_{\alpha}^{R_{s}}\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log(3\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}/\epsilon)\right)}{\tilde{N}}}d\epsilon\leq R_{s}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log\left(3{\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}{N}}\right)\right)}{{N}}}.

Together with the fact that
R_{s}>1/\left(\frac{\xi(\bm{Y})}{N_{C}}\cdot\sqrt{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log(3R_{\mathcal{X}}\cdot\beta\cdot{N})\right)}\right),
we have :

\hat{\mathfrak{R}}_{{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}},\mathcal{S}}(\ell\circ\mathsf{soft}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\beta,\nu})\leq 2{C}\left(\phi_{\ell}\cdot(N_{C}-1)\cdot R_{s}\cdot\xi(\bm{Y})\cdot\sqrt{\frac{N_{par}\left(\nu N_{L}+\beta+\log\left(3{\beta R_{\mathcal{X}}{N}}\right)\right)}{{N}}}\right).

(65)

The proof is completed by choosing
\tilde{C}=2C
.
∎

Appendix G
Efficient Computation

In this section, we will propose acceleration algorithms for loss and gradient evaluation for three well-known losses: exponential loss
\ell_{exp}(\alpha,t)=\exp(-\alpha t)
, squared loss
\ell_{sq}(t)=(\alpha-t)^{2}
, and hinge loss
\ell_{hinge}(\alpha,t)=\max(\alpha-t,0)
.

Generally, the empirical surrogate risk functions
\hat{R}_{\ell}
have the following abstract form:

\hat{R}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})),

\text{where}~~f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=g_{i}(\bm{W}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})),\bm{W}=[\bm{w}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{w}^{(N_{C})}]^{\top}.

We adopt this general form since it covers a lot of popular models. For example, when
g_{i}(\cdot)
is defined as the activation function of the last layer of a neural network (say a softmax function),
\bm{w}^{(i)}
are the weights of the last layer, and
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\cdot)
is the neural net where the last layer is excluded,
f^{(i)}(\bm{x})
becomes a deep neural network architecture with the last layer designed as a fully-connected layer. As for another instance, if both
g_{i}(\bm{x})=\bm{x}
and
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})=\bm{x}
, then we reach a simple linear multiclass scoring function. Note that the scalability with respect to sample size only depends on the choice of
\ell
. The choice of
g_{i}(\cdot)
and
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\cdot)
only affects the instance-wise chain rule. This allows us to provide a general acceleration framework once the surrogate loss is fixed.

Common Notations
. Now we introduce the common notations we adopted in this section. We assume that
\bm{w}^{(i)}
be a vector such that
\bm{w}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{h}\times 1}
. We let
\bm{W}
be the matrix that concatenates
\bm{w}^{(i)}
column-wisely,
i.e.
,

\bm{W}=[\bm{w}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{w}^{(N_{C})}]^{\top},~~\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{C}\times n_{h}}.

(66)

For the sake of convenience, we rearrange
\bm{\theta}
as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}\times 1}
such that it concatenates all the parameters in the model except
\bm{W}
. For example, in a deep neural network,
\bm{\theta}
concatenates all the parameters except the weights of the last layer. Note that this does not affect the result of the loss and gradient evaluation. A simple reshape suffices to transfer our calculation of gradient to its expected result if
\bm{\theta}
has complicated structures.
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})
should be considered as a vector such that
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{h}\times 1}
.
\bm{H}
is then defined as the matrix that concatenates all the
h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})
from every instance in the sense that:

\bm{H}=[h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{N})]^{\top},~~\bm{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{h}\times N}.

(67)

For intermediate partial derivatives, we denote:

\begin{split}\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x})=\frac{\partial(f^{(j)}(\bm{x}))}{\partial\bm{w}^{(i)^{\top}}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})}\end{split}

(68)

Furthermore, we have two compact expressions as:

\begin{split}&\bm{\partial}_{i,j}=[\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{N})]^{\top},~~\bm{\partial}_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 1},\\
&\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})=[\partial_{1}[f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k})],\cdots,\partial_{N_{C}}(f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k}))]^{\top},~~\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{C}\times 1}.\end{split}

(69)

Another variable is also essential when applying the chain rule:

\bm{U}^{(j)}=[\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{1})\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{N})\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{N})],~~\bm{U}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\theta}\times N}.

(70)

Finally, we provide a compact notation of the weights
\frac{1}{{n}_{i}{n}_{j}}
as:

\bm{D}^{(i)}=[\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{y_{1}}},\cdots,\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{y_{N}}}]^{\top},~~\bm{D}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times 1}.

(71)

G-A

Exponential Loss

Loss Evaluation
. For the exponential loss, we can simplify the computations by a factorization scheme:

\begin{split}\hat{R}_{exp}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\exp\left(\alpha\cdot\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)\right),\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\exp(\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\right)}_{(a_{i})}\cdot\underbrace{\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\exp(-\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\right)}_{(b_{i})}\end{split}

From the derivation above, the loss evaluation could be done by first calculating
(a_{i}),(b_{i})
separately and then performing the multiplication, which only takes
O(NN_{C}T_{\ell})
. This is a significant improvement compared with the original
O(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}N_{C}T_{\ell})
result.

Gradient Evaluation
. Due to the factorization scheme, we can also accelerate the gradient evaluation in a similar spirit. According to. Eq.(
66
)-Eq.(
70
), we have:

\begin{split}\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}(a_{j})&=\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\alpha\exp(\alpha\cdot f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{m}))\cdot\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{m})\\
&=\alpha\bm{H}(\mathcal{I}_{i}\odot\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),+}_{exp}\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}).\\
\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}(b_{j})&=-\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\alpha\cdot\exp(-\alpha\cdot f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{n}))\cdot\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{n})\cdot h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{n})\\
&=-\alpha\bm{H}(\mathcal{I}_{\backslash i}\odot\bm{D}^{(i)}\odot\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),-}_{exp}\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}),\\
\nabla_{h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k})}(a_{j})&=\underbrace{\mathcal{I}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\alpha\cdot\exp(\alpha\cdot f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k}))}_{q_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\bm{w}^{(i)},\\
&={q}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\\
\nabla_{h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k})}(b_{j})&=-\underbrace{\mathcal{I}_{j}^{c}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\bm{D}^{(i)}_{k}\cdot\alpha\cdot\exp(-\alpha\cdot f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k}))}_{{\tilde{q}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\bm{w}^{(i)},\\
&=-\tilde{q}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\cdot\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}_{k})\end{split}

where

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}_{i}=I\left[\bm{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}\right],~\mathcal{I}_{\backslash i}=I\left[\bm{x}_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}_{i}\right],~~\mathcal{I}_{j}(\bm{x})=\bm{I}\left[x\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\right],~~\mathcal{I}^{c}_{j}(\bm{x})=1-\mathcal{I}_{j}(\bm{x})\\
&\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),+}_{exp}=[\exp(\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})),\cdots,\exp(\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{N}))]^{\top},\\
&\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),-}_{exp}=[\exp(-\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})),\cdots,\exp(-\alpha\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{N}))]^{\top}.\\
\end{split}

Furthermore, we define
\bm{q}_{j}=[q_{j}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,q_{j}(\bm{x}_{N})]^{\top}

and
\bm{\tilde{q}}_{j}=[\tilde{q}_{j}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,\tilde{q}_{j}(\bm{x}_{N})]^{\top}
. From Eq.(
70
), we have:

\begin{split}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(a_{j})&=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k}))\nabla_{h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k})}a_{j}=\bm{U}^{(j)}\bm{q}_{j}\\
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(b_{j})&=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k}))\nabla_{h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}_{k})}b_{j}=-\bm{U}^{(j)}\bm{\tilde{q}}_{j}\end{split}

Then the gradient evaluation can be done by :

\begin{split}\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}\hat{R}_{\ell}&=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\big(\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}(a_{j})\big)\cdot(b_{j})+(a_{j})\cdot\big(\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}(b_{j})\big)\\
&=\alpha\bm{H}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}b_{j}\mathcal{I}_{i}\odot\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),+}_{exp}\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}-a_{j}\mathcal{I}_{\backslash i}\odot\bm{D}^{(i)}\odot\hat{\bm{y}}^{(i),-}_{exp}\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}\right),\\
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{R}_{\ell}&=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\big(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(a_{j})\big)\cdot(b_{j})+(a_{j})\cdot\big(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}(b_{j})\big)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\left[\bm{U}^{(j)}(b_{j}\bm{q}_{j}-a_{j}\bm{\tilde{q}}_{j})\right].\end{split}

From the compact forms shown above, the acceleration enjoys a
O(NN_{C}T_{grad})
complexity instead of the original
O(N_{C}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}T_{grad})
time.

G-B

Hinge loss

Loss Evaluation
. First we put down the hinge surrogate loss as:

\hat{R}_{hinge}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\left(\alpha-\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)\right)_{+}.

The key of our acceleration is to notice that the terms are non-zero only if
\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)\leq\alpha
. Moreover, for these non-zero terms
\max(x,0)=x
. This means that the hinge loss degenerates to an identity function for the activated nonzero terms, which enjoys efficient computation. So the key step in our algorithm is to find out the non-zero terms in an efficient manner.

Given a fixed class
i
and an instance
\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}
, we denote:

\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})=\{\bm{x}_{n}\notin\mathcal{N}_{i},\alpha>f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\}.

With
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})
, one can reformulate
\hat{R}_{hinge}
as:

\begin{split}\hat{R}_{hinge}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left[\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right)\cdot\left(\alpha-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\right)+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right],\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left[\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot(\alpha-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}))+\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\right],\end{split}

where

\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}},~~\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}).

According to this reformulation, once
\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
and
\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
are all fixed, we can calculate the loss function within
O(N)
. So the efficiency bottleneck comes from the calculations of
\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
and
\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
.

Next we propose an efficient way to calculate
\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}),\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m}),\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})
. To do so, given a specific class
i
, we first sort the relevant (resp. irrelevant) instances descendingly according to their scores
f^{(i)}(\bm{x})
:

\begin{split}&f^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{0})\geq f^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{1})\cdots,\geq f^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{n_{i}-1}),~~~~\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{i}\in\mathcal{N}_{i},\\
&f^{(i)}(\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{0})\geq f^{(i)}(\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{1})\cdots,\geq f^{(i)}(\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{N-n_{i}-1}),~~~~\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{i}\notin\mathcal{N}_{i}.\end{split}

It immediately follows that:

\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{1})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{{n}_{i}-1}).

This allows us to find out all
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k}),k=0,1,\cdots,n_{i}-1,
within a single pass of the whole dataset with an efficient dynamic programming.

Based on the construction of
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k}=\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})
, we provide the following recursive rules to obtain
\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
and
\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x})
:

\begin{split}&\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k+1})=\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k+1}\backslash\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}},\\
&\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k+1})=\Delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k+1}\backslash\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}).\end{split}

These recursive rules induce an efficient dynamic programming which only requires a single pass of the whole dataset. Putting all together, we then come to a
O(N_{C}\bar{N}T_{l})
time complexity speed-up for hinge loss evaluation. An implementation of this idea is detailed in Alg.
1
and Alg.
2
.

Gradient Evaluation
. Similar to the loss evaluation, we can provide an efficient acceleration method for the gradient evaluation as well. Based on a similar analysis, we have:

\begin{split}\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}\hat{R}_{hinge}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left[-\left(\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right]\\
&~~~~=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\left(-\delta^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})+\Gamma^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\right)\\
\end{split}

where

\Gamma^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})=\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}).

For any
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{S}
, and any class
j
, based on Eq.(
66
)-Eq.(
70
), we can calculate
\nabla_{\Theta}f^{(j)}(\bm{x})
from:

\begin{split}&\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}f^{(j)}(\bm{x})=\partial_{i}f^{(j)}(\bm{x})\cdot h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x}),\\
&\nabla_{h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})}f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}),\\
&\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})\bm{W}^{\top}\bm{\partial}_{j}(\bm{x}).\end{split}

We can also adopt a dynamic programming scheme similar to the loss evaluation to speed it up. The first step is exactly the same: obtain
\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{0},\cdots,\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{{n}_{i}-1}
and
\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{0},\cdots,\bm{\tilde{x}}^{\downarrow}_{N-{n}_{i}-1}
with the same method as the loss evaluation and get
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k}=\mathcal{A}^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})
. Then, we have the following recursive expression for the new variable
\Gamma^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})
:

\begin{split}\Gamma^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k+1})=\Gamma^{(i)}(\bm{x}^{\downarrow}_{k})+\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k+1}\backslash\mathcal{A}^{(i)}_{k}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\nabla_{\bm{\Theta}}f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}).\end{split}

Now we can use a similar algorithm to evaluate the gradients, the details are omitted here due to its resemblance to Alg.
2
.

G-C

Squared Loss

Loss Evaluation
.
For each fixed class
i
, we construct an affinity matrix
\bm{Aff}^{(i)}
such that

\bm{Aff}^{(i)}_{m,n}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{n_{i}n_{y_{m}}},&n\in\mathcal{N}_{i},m\notin\mathcal{N}_{i},\\
\dfrac{1}{n_{i}n_{y_{n}}},&m\in\mathcal{N}_{i},n\notin\mathcal{N}_{i},\\
0,&\text{Otherwise},\end{cases}

which could be written in a matrix form,

\bm{Aff}^{(i)}=\bm{D}^{(i)}({{\mathbf{1}}}-{{\mathbf{Y}}^{(i)}}){{\mathbf{Y}}^{(i)}}^{\top}+{{\mathbf{Y}}^{(i)}}{{({{\mathbf{1}}}-{{\mathbf{Y}}^{(i)}})}^{\top}}\bm{D}^{(i)},

Then the empirical risk function under the squared surrogate loss could be reformulated as:

\hat{R}_{sq}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)^{\top}}_{sq}\mathcal{L}^{(i)}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq},

where

\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq}=\bm{Y}^{(i)}-f^{(i)}(\bm{X}),~~f^{(i)}(\bm{X})=[f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1}),\cdots,f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{N})]^{\top},

(72)

and
\mathcal{L}^{(i)}=diag(\bm{Aff}^{(i)}\bm{1})-\bm{Aff}^{(i)}
is the graph Laplacian matrix associated with
\bm{Aff}^{(i)}
. Based on this reformulation, one can find that the structure of
\mathcal{L}^{(i)}
provides an efficient factorization scheme to speed-up loss evaluation. To see this, we have:

\hat{R}_{sq}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)\top}_{sq}(\bm{\kappa}^{(i)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq})-{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\cdot{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2},

where

\begin{split}&{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}=\bm{Y}^{(i)^{\top}}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq}\\
&{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}=\bm{\Delta}^{(i)^{\top}}_{sq}\bm{D}^{(i)}(\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})\\
&\bm{\kappa}^{(i)}={n}_{i}\bm{D}^{(i)}(\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})+\dfrac{N_{C}-1}{{n}_{i}}\bm{Y}^{(i)}.\end{split}

Obviously both terms in the equation above require only
O(N_{C}NT_{l})
time. We summarize this factorization scheme in Alg.
3
.

Algorithm 3

CalSqLoss

1:

\bm{X},\bm{Y},\alpha,\varphi(\cdot)
.

2:

loss
.

3:

\texttt{loss}\leftarrow 0

4:

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

N_{C}

do

5:

Obtain
\bm{D}^{(i)}

6:

\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}\leftarrow(\bm{Y}^{(i)}-f^{(i)}(\bm{X}))

7:

\bm{\kappa}^{(i)}\leftarrow{n}_{i}\bm{D}^{(i)}(\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})+\dfrac{N_{C}-1}{{n}_{i}}\bm{Y}^{(i)}

8:

{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\leftarrow\left(\bm{\Delta}^{(i)^{\top}}_{sq}(\bm{D}^{(i)}\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})\right)

9:

{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}\leftarrow\bm{Y}^{(i)^{\top}}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq}

10:

\texttt{loss}+=\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)\top}_{sq}(\bm{\kappa}^{(i)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq})-{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\cdot{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}

11:

end

for

Gradient Evaluation
. From Alg.
3
, Eq.(
66
)-Eq.(
70
) and the chain rule, we have:

\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}\hat{R}_{sq}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\nabla_{\bm{\bm{w}}^{(i)}}\bm{\Delta}^{(i)\top}_{sq}(\bm{\kappa}^{(i)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(i)}_{sq})-\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\cdot{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}

where

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}\bm{\Delta}^{(j)^{\top}}_{sq}(\bm{\kappa}^{(j)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(j)}_{sq})&=\bm{H}(\bm{\Delta}^{(j)}_{sq}\odot\bm{\kappa}^{(j)}\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}),\\
\nabla_{\bm{w}^{(i)}}{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}&=\bm{H}\left[\left({\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}\cdot\bm{D}^{(i)}(\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})+{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\bm{Y}^{(i)}\right)\odot\bm{\partial}_{i,j}\right],\\
\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\bm{\Delta}^{(j)^{\top}}_{sq}(\bm{\kappa}^{(j)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(j)}_{sq})&=\bm{U}^{(j)}(\kappa^{(j)}\odot\bm{\Delta}^{(j)}),\\
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}&=\bm{U}^{(j)}\left[{\Delta}^{(i)}_{2}\cdot\bm{D}^{(i)}(\bm{1}-\bm{Y}^{(i)})+{\Delta}^{(i)}_{1}\bm{Y}^{(i)}\right],\end{split}

Again this shows that the gradient evaluation could be done within
O(N_{C}NT_{grad})
time.

G-D

Acceleration Scheme for general losses

Unfortunately, the lossless acceleration for general losses is impossible in general. In this subsection, following the spirit of
[
75
]
, we provide a discussion on how to accelerate general loss functions approximately. However, we can, in turn, construct an approximation framework based on the Bernstein Polynomials. Note that the key difference between our work and
[
75
]
is that we do not need the minimax reformulation. Moreover, we also present a nice property of such approximations in terms of consistency.

First of all, we define the Berstein Polynomials as the following.

Definition 7
.

The Berstein Polynomials of degree
m
of the function
\varphi:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are defined, for any
u\in[0,1]
, by

\mathbb{B}_{m}(\varphi,u)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varphi\left(\frac{k}{m}\right)\cdot\binom{m}{k}\cdot u^{k}\cdot(1-u)^{m-k}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\cdot\Delta^{k}\varphi(0)\cdot u^{k}.

(73)

where
\Delta^{k}\varphi(0)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-j}\tbinom{k}{j}\varphi\left(\frac{j}{m}\right)
is the forward difference operator on
\varphi
at 0.

Note that the domain of the scoring functions
f^{(i)}
are restricted to
[0,1]
, by choosing
\varphi(u)=\ell(2s-1)
, we have:

\begin{split}\ell(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n}))&\approx\mathbb{B}_{K}\left(\varphi,\frac{1+f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})}{2}\right)\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\binom{m}{k}\Delta^{k}\varphi(0)\left(\varphi,\frac{1+f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})}{2}\right)^{k}\\
&=\frac{1}{K+1}\sum_{k=0}^{m}f^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{n})\end{split}

(74)

where

\begin{split}&f^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{m})=\left(\frac{1}{2}+f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})\right)^{k}\\
&f^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{n})=\sum_{j=k}^{m}\binom{m}{j}\cdot\binom{j}{k}\cdot\frac{(m+1)\cdot\Delta^{k}\varphi(0)}{2^{j}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)^{j-k}.\end{split}

With the Bernstein Polynomials, we now construct generic acceleration methods for surrogate risk computation.

Acceleration for General Loss Calculation
. Through the lens of the Bernstein polynomials, the loss function could be approximated by the following derivation:

\begin{split}\hat{R}_{\ell}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)\\
&\approx\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\frac{1}{K+1}\sum_{k=0}^{K}f^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{n})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{K+1}\bm{e}_{+}^{(i)\top}\bm{e}_{-}^{(i)}\\
&\triangleq\hat{R}^{K}_{\ell}\end{split}

where

\begin{split}\bm{e}_{+}^{(i)}&=\left[\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{f^{(i)}_{1}(\bm{x}_{m})}{n_{i}},\cdots,\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\frac{f^{(i)}_{K}(\bm{x}_{m})}{n_{i}}\right]^{\top},\\
\bm{e}_{-}^{(i)}&=\left[\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{1}(\bm{x}_{n})}{n_{j}},\cdots,\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{K}(\bm{x}_{n})}{n_{j}}\right]^{\top}.\end{split}

Obviously, it only requires
O(K\cdot N_{C}\cdot N)
time to finish the loss computation.

Acceleration for General Gradient Calculation
. Similarly, the gradient calculation could be approximated by the following derivation:

\begin{split}\nabla\hat{R}^{K}_{\ell}&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\nabla\ell\left(f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{m})-f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{n})\right)\\
&\approx\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{\bm{x}_{m}\in\mathcal{N}_{i}}\sum_{j\neq i}\sum_{\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}_{j}}\frac{1}{n_{i}n_{j}}\cdot\frac{1}{K+1}\sum_{k=0}^{K}f^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{m})\cdot\nabla\tilde{f}^{(i)}_{k}(\bm{x}_{n})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\frac{1}{K+1}(\nabla\bm{e}_{+}^{(i)})^{\top}\bm{e}_{-}^{(i)}+\bm{e}_{+}^{(i)\top}(\nabla\bm{e}_{-}^{(i)})\end{split}

\nabla\bm{e}_{+}^{(i)}
and
\nabla\bm{e}_{-}^{(i)}
could be obtained in a similar spirit to the calculation of the exponential loss. It thus requires
O(K\cdot N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{grad})
time to finish the gradient computation.

Consistency of the Approximated Loss function
Now, we proceed to present an elegant property of the approximated risk functions. Specifically, we show that
\hat{R}^{K}_{\ell}

is, in itself, a consistent loss function if
\ell
is consistent. To do this, we need the following theorem about the Bernstein polynomials.

Theorem 10
.

If
f\in C^{k}[0,1]
, for some
k\geq 0
, then :

m\leq\frac{d^{k}(f(x))}{dx^{k}}\leq M,~\Rightarrow~c_{k}\cdot m\leq\frac{d^{k}(\mathbb{B}_{K}(f,x))}{dx^{k}}\leq c_{k}\cdot M

Then the following corollary gives the final result.

Corollary 2
.

For all surrogate loss function
\ell\in C^{2}[0,1]
,

\hat{R}_{\ell}~\text{is}~{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}~\text{consistent}~\Rightarrow\hat{R}^{K}_{\ell}~\text{is}~{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}~\text{consistent}~

Proof
.

Again, by choosing
\varphi(u)=\ell(2s-1)
, we have
\varphi(u)\in C^{2}[0,1]
. The result follows directly from Thm.
10
, and Thm.
3
.
∎

G-E

Summary and Concluding Remarks

TABLE VI:
Acceleration for three losses , where
\bar{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}{n}_{i}\log{n}_{i}+(N-{n}_{i})\log(N-{n}_{i})

Algorithms

loss

gradient

requirement

exp + acceleration

O(N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{\ell})

O(N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{grad})

\min_{i}{n}_{i}\gg 2

squared + acceleration

O(N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{\ell})

O(N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{grad})

e^{\frac{1}{2}(N-{n}_{i})}\gg{n}_{i}\gg N-e^{\frac{1}{2}{n}_{i}}

hinge + acceleration

O(N_{C}\cdot\bar{N}\cdot T_{\ell})

O(N_{C}\cdot\bar{N}\cdot T_{grad})

\min_{i}{n}_{i}\gg 2

general + acceleration

O(K\cdot N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{\ell})

O(K\cdot N_{C}\cdot N\cdot T_{grad})

e^{\frac{1}{2}(N-{n}_{i})}\gg{n}_{i}\gg N-e^{\frac{1}{2}{n}_{i}}

w/o acceleration

O(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}\cdot T_{\ell})

O(\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}\cdot T_{grad})

\backslash

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}-N_{C}\cdot N=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\left[n_{i}(N-{n}_{i})-({n}_{i}+(N-{n}_{i}))\right].

To ensure
N_{C}\cdot N<\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}
, one must let

n_{i}(N-{n}_{i})>({n}_{i}+(N-{n}_{i})),~~~\forall i.

Obviously, this requirement could be met when
\min_{i}{n}_{i}\gg 2
. Similarly, we have:

\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}-N_{C}\cdot\bar{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\left[n_{i}(N-{n}_{i})-{n}_{i}\log({n}_{i})-(N-{n}_{i})\log(N-{n}_{i})\right].

In this sense
N_{C}\cdot\bar{N}<\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}
could be satisfied if

\frac{1}{2}(N-{n}_{i})>\log({n}_{i}),~~\frac{1}{2}({n}_{i})>\log(N-{n}_{i}),~~\forall i.

This means that

\exp(\frac{1}{2}(N-{n}_{i}))\gg{n}_{i}\gg N-\exp(\frac{1}{2}{n}_{i}),~~\forall i.

should be guaranteed to ensure a significant improvement. It is easy to see that all the requirements are essentially loose restricts on the data distribution which could be satisfied for the majority of real-world datasets. As another remark, we do not provide a detailed calculation of
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}h_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{x})
, since it follows the chain rule and could be obtained from off-the-shelf toolboxes such as tensorflow and pytorch where auto differentiation is supported. Moreover, our acceleration schemes are based either on changing the order of operations or on reformulating the original loss. Hence, when the model is implemented in such auto differentiation platforms, the gradient acceleration does not need to be explicitly implemented. One just needs to implement the accelerated loss evaluation to fix the computational graph, the gradient evaluation then follows the graph naturally.

Appendix H
Experiments

H-A

Numerical Validations for the Acceleration Algorithms

Fig. 3:
Acceleration Ratio vs. Sample Scale

Setting
. As a warm-up experiment, we first validate the effectiveness of our proposed speed-up methods detailed in Sec.
VI
. Note that our algorithms only concern loss and gradient evaluations, which do not depend on the optimization algorithms in question. Hence, to be fair, we will perform stand-alone running time tests where no optimization algorithms are involved. Following this spirit, our comparisons are based on the results from 30 repetitions of pure loss and gradient evaluations. To do this, we randomly generate a series of dataset
\bm{Z}_{i}=(\bm{X}_{i},\bm{Y}_{i}),~i=1,2,\cdots,5
, where
\bm{X}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{k}\times d},\bm{Y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{i}\times N_{C}}
are the input feature matrix and the output one-hot label matrix. Specifically, we set
N_{i}=\{32,64,128,256,512,1024\},~d=100,~N_{C}=5
.
\bm{X}_{i}
is generated from the uniform distribution within
[0,1]
. Denote
\rho_{i}=\nicefrac{{{n}_{i}}}{{N}}
, then
\bm{Y}
is generated such that
\rho_{1}=0.2,\rho_{2}=0.1,\rho_{3}=0.2,\rho_{4}=0.4,\rho_{5}=0.1
. Moreover, we fix a linear scoring function
f(\cdot)
with softmax output
f^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\mathsf{softmax}(\bm{W}^{(i)}\bm{x})
, where the weight matrix
\bm{W}=[\bm{W}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{W}^{(5)}]
is generated in the same way as
\bm{X}
. Then we test the running time of the naive evaluation algorithm and our speed-up algorithms respectively over different
\bm{Z}_{i}
.

Results and Analysis
. To show the efficiency improvements, we plot the mean of the acceleration ratio in Fig.
3
, which is defined as:

\begin{split}&\text{acceleration ratio}\\
&=\frac{\text{Running time of the naive method}}{\text{Running time of the acceleration method}}.\end{split}

over 30 repetitions against the scale of the datasets. We have the following two findings: (a) The proposed algorithms generate significant speed-up over all sample scales being tested.
They produce
10\sim 100
x speed-up when the sample size remains as small as 32 while the result gradually climbs-up as the sample size enlarges and finally reaches
10000
x speed-up for the squared and the exponential loss when the sample size becomes 1024. This shows that the proposed algorithms could both work for mini-batch-based and full-batch-based optimization methods. (b) The curves suggest that the acceleration ratios are roughly scaled as
O(N)
, which is consistent with the complexity analysis shown in Tab.
VI
. To see this, for squared and exponential loss, we have:

\begin{split}\text{acceleration ratio}&\approx\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}n_{i}n_{j}}{N_{C}\cdot N}\\
&=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\rho_{i}\cdot\rho_{j}}{N_{C}}\cdot N\end{split}

Note that we keep
\rho_{i}
and
N_{C}
as constants in our experiment, this suggests that the acceleration ratio should be
O(N)
. For hinge loss, there are extra factors from
\log{n}_{i}
and
\log(N-{n}_{i})
. However, these factors mostly remain at a constant level considering that the number of samples being tested is not large. This is why we also observe an almost linear scale-up of the acceleration ratio of hinge loss. Moreover, for hinge loss, we see that the increase of the acceleration ratio becomes slower for the gradient evaluations when the number of samples surpasses 500. A proper reason for this phenomenon is that the evaluation algorithms for hinge loss are partially implemented by
Cython
. For a large scale dataset, the contribution of a
Cython
implementation should be more significant for loss than for gradient. This is because that the matrix operations in gradient evaluation could be accelerated by proper and compact use of
numpy
even without
Cython
, while the loss evaluations, containing mostly elementary operations, enjoy a shaper speed-up from
Cython
.

Here, we provide a more detailed description for traditional datasets from LIBSVM and KEEL.

(a)

LIBSVM Datasets
.
https://www.csie.ntu.edu.tw/˜cjlin/libsvmtools/datasets/

•

Abalone
\star
. The task of this dataset is to predict the age of abalone from physical measurements. The features include basic information such as the gender, length, height of a given subject. To construct a multi-class problem from this dataset. We regard each possible age as a class. On top of this, we merge the first three classes, and we also merge all the classes greater than 20.

•

Shuttle
. The shuttle dataset contains 9 features all of which are numerical. The first one being time. The last column is the class which has been coded as follows: 1 Rad Flow,,2 Fpv Close,3 Fpv Open,4 High,5 Bypass,6 Bpv Close,7 Bpv Open.

•

Svmguide-2
.
[
34
]
released this dataset when the authors are providing a guidance for the
LIBSVM
library. This dataset comes from a bacteria protein subcellular localization dataset which was proposed in
[
26
]
.

(b)

KEEL Datasets
.
http://www.keel.es/

•

Balance
. This data set was generated to model psychological experimental results. Each example is classified as having the balance scale tip to the right, tip to the left, or be balanced. The attributes are the left weight, the left distance, the right weight, and the right distance.

•

Dermatology
. The differential diagnosis of erythemato-squamous diseases is a real problem in dermatology. They all share the clinical features of erythema and scaling, with few differences. The diseases in this group are psoriasis, seborrheic dermatitis, lichen planus, pityriasis rosea, chronic dermatitis, and pityriasis rubra pilaris. Usually a biopsy is necessary for the diagnosis, but, unfortunately, these diseases share many histopathological features as well. Another difficulty for the differential diagnosis is that a disease may show the features of another disease at the beginning stage and may have the characteristic features at the following stages. Patients were first evaluated clinically with 12 features. Afterwards, skin samples were taken for the evaluation of 22 histopathological features. The values of the histopathological features are determined by an analysis of the samples under a microscope. In the dataset constructed for this domain, the family history feature has the value 1 if any of these diseases has been observed in the family, and 0 otherwise. The age feature simply represents the age of the patient. Every other feature (clinical and histopathological) was given a degree in the range of 0 to 3. Here, 0 indicates that the feature was not presented, 3 indicates the largest amount possible, and 1, 2 indicate the relative intermediate values.

•

Ecoli
. The task of this dataset is to predict protein localization sites, where the features are given as a series of scores evaluated by standard detection methods.

•

Glass
. A Glass type identification dataset with 9 features.

•

New Thyroid
. This is a Thyroid Disease (New Thyroid) dataset with three classes: normal hyper and hypo.

•

Page Blocks
. The task of this dataset is to classify all the blocks of the page layout of a document that has been detected by a segmentation process, where each of the observed blocks has 10 features describing its basic information.

•

Wine
. A Wine classification dataset where the model should classify a given data point to one of the types of wines based on 13 dimensions of features.

(c)

Other Datasets
:

(1)

CIFAR-100-Imb
. We sampled an imbalanced version of the CIFAR-100
9
9

9

https://www.cs.toronto.edu/~kriz/cifar.html
dataset, which originally contains 100 image classes each with 600 instances. The 100 classes are encoded as
1,\cdots 100
. For each class
i
, we randomly sampled
n_{i}
instances shown in Tab.
IX
to gain an imbalanced version of the dataset.

(2)

User-Imb
. The original dataset is collected from TalkingData, a famous third-party mobile data platform from China, which predicts mobile users’ demographic characteristics based on their app usage records. The dataset is collected for the Kaggle Competition named Talking Data Mobile User Demographics.
10
10

10

https://www.kaggle.com/c/talkingdata-mobile-user-demographics/data
The raw features include logged events, app attributes, and device information. There are 12 target classes ’F23-’, ’F24-26’,’F27-28’,’F29-32’, ’F33-42’, ’F43+’, ’M22-’, ’M23-26’, ’M27-28’, ’M29-31’, ’M32-38’, ’M39+’, which describe the demographics (gender and age) of users. In our experiments, we sample an imbalanced subset of the original dataset to leverage a class-skewed dataset. The number of instances in each class after resampling is listed as Tab.
VIII
.

(3)

iNaturalist2017.
iNaturalist Challenge 2017 dataset
11
11

11

https://www.kaggle.com/c/inaturalist-challenge-at-fgvc-2017
is a large-scale image classification benchmark with 675,170 images covering 5,089 different species of plants and animals. We split the dataset into the training set, validation set, and test set at a ratio of 0.7:0.15:0.15. Since directly training deep models in such a large-scale dataset is time-consuming, we instead generate 2048-d features with a ResNet-50 model pre-trained on ImageNet for each image and train models with three fully-connected layers for all methods. We utilize Adam optimizer to train the models, with an initial learning rate of
10^{-5}
. To ensure all categories are covered in a mini-batch, the batch size is set to 8196. Other hyperparameters are the same as those in the CIFAR-100-Imb dataset.

H-C

Implementation Details

Infrastructure
. All the experiments are carried out on a ubuntu 16.04.6 server equipped with Intel(R) Xeon(R) CPU E5-2620 v4 cpu and a TITAN RTX GPU. The codes are implemented via
python 3.6.7
, the basic dependencies are:
pytorch
(v-1.1.0),
sklearn
(v-0.21.3),
numpy
(v-1.16.2). For traditional datasets, we implement our proposed algorithms with the help of the
sklearn
and
numpy
. For hinge loss, we use Cython to accelerate the dynamic programming algorithm. For the deep learning datasets, our proposed algorithms are implemented with
pytorch
.

Evaluation Metric
. Given a trained scoring function
f=(f^{(1)},\cdots,f^{(N_{C})})
, all the forthcoming results are evaluated with reward reversion of MAUC, where we denote it as
\mathsf{MAUC}^{\uparrow}
:

\mathsf{MAUC}^{\uparrow}=\frac{1}{N_{C}\cdot(N_{C}-1)}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N_{C}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\left|\left\{(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{2}):\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{N}_{i},~\bm{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{j},f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{1})>f^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{2})\right\}\right|}{{n}_{i}{n}_{j}}

in this subsection.

Traditional Datasets
. For all the experiments, hyper-parameters are tuned based on the training and validation set while the performances are evaluated over a test set, where the training, valid, and test set accounts for
80\%,~10\%,~10\%
of the original dataset, respectively. To remain the label distribution of the overall dataset, the dataset splits are generated with a stratified sampling strategy with respect to classes. The experiments are done with 15 repetitions for each involved algorithm. Since all the datasets fall in this class contains simple features, we use a linear model composited with a softmax transformation as the scoring function. More formally, given an instance
\bm{x}
, we have
f(\bm{x})=\mathsf{softmax}(\bm{W}\bm{x})
, where
\bm{W}=[\bm{\omega}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{\omega}^{(N_{C})}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times N_{C}}
is the weight of the linear function. For the sampling-based competitors, the corresponding sampling algorithm is first performed on the dataset, then a scoring function is trained based on the multiclass cross-entropy loss. For LR, the scoring function is directly trained based on the multiclass cross-entropy loss without sampling. For our proposed algorithms, we directly wrap the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
surrogate losses on the scoring function
f
and perform the training process without any simpling procedure. The hyper-parameters we adopted to produce the performance of our algorithms are shown in Tab.
XII
.

Deep Learning Datasets
. For all the experiments, hyper-parameters are tuned based on the training and validation set while the performances are evaluated over a test set, where the training, valid, and test set accounts for
80\%,~10\%,~10\%
of the original dataset, respectively. To remain the label distribution of the overall dataset, the dataset splits are generated with a stratified sampling strategy with respect to classes. For these two datasets, all the models are constructed with a deep neural network. The scoring functions share a common form as
f(\bm{x})=\mathsf{softmax}(fc_{2}(fc_{1}(\bm{x})))
, where
fc_{1},fc_{2}
are fully-connected layers. For CIFAR-100-Imb, we adopt the ResNet-50 pre-trained features from
layer4
after average pooling as the input for each method. For User-Imb, the models are trained from scratch. The architecture we use for CIFAR-100-Imb and User-Imb are shown in Tab.
XII
and Tab.
XII
, respectively. For the sampling-based competitors, the corresponding sampling algorithm is first performed on the dataset, then a scoring function is trained based on the multiclass cross-entropy loss together with a three-layered deep neural network. For LR, the scoring function is directly trained based on the multiclass cross-entropy loss and the neural net without sampling. For our proposed algorithms, we directly wrap the
{\mathsf{MAUC}^{\downarrow}}
surrogate losses on the neural net
f
and perform the training process without any simpling procedure. The hyper-parameters we adopted to produce the performance of our algorithms are shown in Tab.
XIII
and Tab.
XIV
, respectively for CIFAR-100-Imb and User-Imb.
